# Athanasius of Alexandria, Second Oration against the Arians (Athanasius of Alexandria, Oratio II contra Arianos)

**Author**: Athanasius of Alexandria  
**Series**: PO Tome 56  
**Columns**: pp. 419–741  

[p. 419] {#po.56.p419}
свѧтААгО А·ѳАнАсА АРхїЕпискꙋпА
Archbishop of Alexandria
александрьскааго. слово второє. на арианы: Τοῦ αὐτοῦ κατὰ Ἀρειανῶν λόγος δεύτερος


[p. 421] {#po.56.p421}
*Аꙁъ оубо мнѣхъ ꙗко сѫштєи отъ ариєва
1. нєистоlвьства лицємѣри. О прєдърєчєнъими прьвѣте сєго обличєниими. и о оукаꙁаниихъ истинънъихъ довъльни бѫдѫть. и прѣстаѭштє оужє ꙁаꙁьрѣти имѫть сєбѣ. О нихⸯжє съмыслишѧ жє и глаголашѧ ꙁълѣ о сынѣ самиⷧ нє вѣдѣ како. ни тако нє срамлѧѭтьсѧ. нꙑ ꙗко свиниѧ и пьси, о своихъ блъвотинахъ. и въ своеи тинѣ калѧѭштєсѧ пачⷧє иꙁобрѣтаѭть жє сєбѣ оумышлєниѧ въ нєчьстие.| 1. Ἐγὼ μὲν φῴμην τοὺς τῆς Ἀρείου μανίας ὑποκριτὰς ἐπὶ τοῖς προειρημένοις πρὸ τούτου κατ’ αὑτῶν ἐλέγχοις, καὶ ταῖς περὶ τῆς ἀληθείας ἀποδείξεσιν ἀρκεῖσθαι, καὶ παυομένους λοιπὸν, μεταγινώσκειν ἐφ’ οἷς ἐφρόνησάν τε καὶ ἐλάλησαν κακῶς περὶ τοῦ Σωτῆρος· αὐτοὶ δὲ, οὐκ οἷδ᾽ ὅπως, οὐδὲ οὕτως καταδύονται· ἀλλ᾽ ὡς χοῖροι καὶ κύνες περὶ τὰ ἵδια ἐξεράματα καὶ τὸν ἔαυτῶν βόρβορον κυλιόμενοι, μᾶλλον ἐφευρίσκουσιν ἑαυτοῖς ἐπινοίας εἰς ἀσέβειαν.

Now, | did think that in view of the refutations and arguments of truth pointed out previously, the hypocrites of Arius’ madness would be satisfied and stop after being rebuked for the evil they made up and spoke concerning the Son. Yet, for a reason unknown to me, they not only feel no shame, but like “pigs” and “dogs wallowing in their vomit” (2 Pet 2.22) and in their mud, rather devise for themselves impious schemes.
раꙁоумѣштє же ни напсаноє въ притчѧхⷽ · г҃ь съꙁда мѧ начѧтъкъ пѫтии своихъ въ дѣлєⷯ єго. ни рєчєноє апостоломъ вѣръна сѫшта сътворьшоуоумѫ єго · прѧть беіꙁ оума глаголѭштє сътворєнїє и ꙁъданїе сѫштꙁа божиѧ сына. Μὴ νοοῦντες γοῦν μήτε τὸ ἐν ταῖς Παροιμίαις γεγραμμένον Κύριος ἔκτισέ με ἀρχὴν όδῶν αὐτοῦ εἰς ἔργα αὐτοῦ, μήτε τὸ παρὰ τοῦ Ἀποστόλου εἰρημένον, Πιστὸν ὅντα τῷ ποιήσαντι αὐτὸν φιλονεικοῦσιν ἁπλῶς, λέγοντες ποίημα καὶ κτίσμα εῖναι τὸν τοῦ Θεοῖ

Κτισμάτων γὰρ
бо и сътворєниѧ присньно єсть. єжє глаголатисѧ отъ не сѫштий.| * и не бѣ прьвие дажє не родисѧ. нъ ꙁанєжє ꙗкожє боєштєсѧ, отъстѫпити отъ своеѧ блѧди. непьштєваѭть по обычѧѭ. прѣⷣрєчєнаѧ словєса божииⷯ книгъ. написанаѧ добрѣ. блѧдиѭ жє сътворєна ими. єштєжє пакы вꙑсприимъшє оумъ прѣдърєчєныиⷯ, вѣрьныихы въспомѧнємъ. сихъ жє обличимъ и когожⷣо тѣхъ, никакоⷤ не вѣдѫштѧ крьстиꙗньство.| καὶ ποιημάτων ἴδιον τὸ λέγεσθαι ἐξ οὐκ ὅντων, καὶ, Οὐκ ἦν πρὶν γεννηθῆ· ἀλλ’ ἐπειδὴ, ὥσπερ δεδιότες ἀποστῆναι τῆς ἱ̓δίας μυθοπλαστίας, προφασίζονται συνήθως τὰ προειρημένα ῥητὰ τῶν θείων Γραφῶν, τὰ καλῶς μὲν γεγραμμένα, ῥαδιουργηθέντα δὲ παρ αὐτῶν φέρε, πάλιν ἐπαναλαβόντες τὸν νοῦν τῶν προειρημένων, τοὺς μὲν πιστοὺς ὑπομνήσωμεν, τούτους δὲ δείξωμεν καὶ ἐξ ἐκάστου τούτων, μηδ᾽ ὅλως εἰδέναι τὸν Χριστιανισμόν.

Аштє оубо нѣсты сынъ. да наричєтьсѧ и тварь. всє же- єже и о твари хоулимо бѫди и на нємъ. и тъкмо нє бѫди ричємъ сынъ, ни слово, ни прѣмѫдроість. и самъ жє б҃ъ, не ричємъ сынъ, ни слово, ни прѣмѫдроість. и самъ же б҃ъ, нє бѫди ричємъ отьць.| нъ тъчиѭ съдѣтєль, и ꙁиждитєль бѫди ричємъ ѻтьць.! нъ тъчиѭ съдѣтєль, и ꙁиждитєль бываѭштииⷯ имъ. и бѫди тварь же Обраꙁы и наічєртаниє, съдѣтєльнааго съвѣта єго; самъже по онѣ, не бѫди рожⷧствьнааго сѫтіства. ꙗко да своего єго єстьства. не быlти словєси ни прѣмѫдрости; ни отъноуⷧ ѡбраꙁѫ. аштє бо нѣсть сынъ, ни ѡбраꙁъ. 2. Εἰ μὲν οὖν οὐκ ἔστιν Yἱὸς, λεγέσθω καὶ ποίημα· πάντα τε τὰ ἐπὶ τῶν ποιημάτων κατηγορείσθω καὶ ἐπ’ αὐτοῦ· καὶ μόνος μὴ λεγέσθω Yἱός· μὴ Λόγος, μὴ Σοφία· καὶ αὐτὸς δὲ ὁ Θεὸς μὴ λεγέσθω Πατὴρ, ἀλλὰ μόνον δημιουργὸς καὶ κτίστης τῶν γινομένων ὑπʼ αὐτοῦ Καὶ ἔστω ἡ μὲν κτίσις, εἰκὼν καὶ χαρακτὴρ τῆς δημιουργικῆς βουλήσεως αὐτοῦ· αὐτὸς δὲ κατʼ ἐκείνους μὴ ἔστω γεννητικῆς φύσεως· ὥστε τῆς ἰδίας οὐσίας αὐτοῦ μὴ εῖναι Λόγον, μὴ Σοφίαν, μηδ ὅλως εἰκόνα. Εἰ γὰρ μή ἐστιν Yἱὸς, οὐδὲ εἰκών.
2. They understand neither what is written in the Proverbs, “The Lord has created me a beginning of his ways for his works” (Prov 8.22), nor what is said by the apostle, “Who was faithful to the one who made him” (Heb 3.2), arguing mindlessly and saying that God's Son is a work and a creature.

бываѧѭшта бываѭть беⷥ сєгожє ничьсожє нє бѫдєть. не имать жє по васъ о нємъжє и имже вс̾є творить · аштє оубо нє породно єсть. само божиє естьство. * нъ поусто по ниⷯ ꙗкожє свѣтъ нє свѣштаѧ. и источьникъ сѫхъ. како съдѣтєльно дѣиство его имѣти глаголѭштє, нє стыдѧтьсѧ. и расыпаѭштєⷩ єжє по сѫтьствоу. како хотѧштє прѣдълагати єжє по воли, не срамлѧтьсѧ. аштє жє вънѣшнѧѧ и не сѫшта прьвѣє. хотѧ жє да сѫть сътварѧєть. и бываєть ихъ творєць. вєльми прьвѣє бѫдєть сєгда отць порода отъ своєго єстьства· οὐκ ἄν τι γένοιτο, οὐκ ἔχει δὲ καθ’ ὑμᾶς ἐν ᾦ καὶ δι’ οὖ τὰ πάντα ποιεῖ; Εἰ δὲ μὴ καρπογόνος ἐστὶν αὐτὴ ἡ θεία οὐσία, ἀλλ’ ἔρημος, κατ αὐτοὺς, ὡς φῶς μὴ φωτίζον, καὶ πηγὴ ξηρὰ, πῶς δημιουργικὴν ἐνέργειαν ἔχειν αὐτὸν λέγοντες οὒκ αἰσχύνονται; Καὶ ἀναιροῦντες δε τὸ κατὰ φύσιν, πῶς τὸ κατὰ βούλησιν προηγεῖσθαι θέλοντες οὐκ ἐρυθριῶσιν; Εἰ δὲ τὰ ἐκτὸς καὶ οὐκ ὅντα πρότερον, βουλόμενος δε αὐτὰ εἶναι, δημιουργεῖ, καὶ γίνεται τούτων ποιητής· πολλῷ πρότερον εἷη ἂν πατὴρ γεννήματος ἒκ τῆς ἱδίας οὐσίας.
2. 1. If then he is not a Son, let him be called a work. And let everything that refers to works be also (used) to profane him; and let him not be called solely Son, nor Word, nor Wisdom; and let God himself not be called Father, but only framer and creator of things that came to be by him. And let the work be image and expression of his framing counsel, and let him, as they hold, be without a generative nature, so that there would be neither Word, nor Wisdom, nor any image whatsoever proper to his essence. For if he is not Son, he is not image either.


[p. 427] {#po.56.p427}
аштє бо хотѣниє ѻ не сѫштииⷯ даѭть богови. почто прѣвышынеє съвѣта божиаго нє поꙁнаваѭть прѣвъꙁидє бо хотѣниѧ єже бывъшѫ и сѫштꙋ ємоу Отьцоу своего словєсє. аштє оубо прьвоє єже єсть по сѫтьствоу. не бѣѧшє по беꙁоуми онѣхъ, како второе єже Єсть по воли боудєⷢ҇. пръвѣє оубо єсть слово. и второє тварь. Εἰ γὰρ τὸ βούλεσθαι περὶ τῶν μὴ ὅντων διδόασι τῷ Θεῷ, διὰ τί μη τὸ ὑπερκείμενον τῆς βουλήσεως οὐκ ἐπιγινώσκουσι τοῦ Θεοῦ Υπεραναβέβηκε δὲ τῆς βουλήσεως τὸ πεφυκέναι καὶ εῖναι αὐτὸν πατέρα τοῦ ἰδίου Λόγου. Εἰ τοίνυν τὸ πρότερον, ὅπερ ἐστὶ κατὰ φύσιν οὐχ ὑπῆρξε κατὰ τὴν ἐκείνων ἄνοιαν, πῶς τὸ δεύτερον, ὅπερ ἐστὶ κατὸ βούλησιν, γένοιτ’ ἄν; Πρότερον δέ ἐστιν ὁ Λόγος, καὶ δεύτερονι κτισις.
нъ єсть слово∙ аштє и большьми дръꙁаѭιть нєчьстивии. тѣмъ бо бысть тварь. и ꙗвѣ жє єсть. ꙗко творьць сы б҃ъ. имать и съдѣтєлноє слово. не иꙁъвнѫ, нъ своє себѣ. Пакы бо тождє Ἀλλ᾽ ἔστιν ὁ Λόγος, κὰν πλείονα τολμῶσιν οἱ ἀσεβεῖς· δι’ αύτοῦ γὰρ γέγονεν ἥ κτίσις καὶ δῆλον ἂν εἷῃ, ὅτι, ποιητὴς ὣν ὁ Θεὸς, ἔχει καὶ τὸν δημιουργικὸν Λόγον οὐκ ἔξωθεν, ἀλλ᾽ ἴδιον ἑαυτοῦ· πάλιν γὰρ
2. But (if) he is not Son, how then do you say God is creator? For if all things that exist come into being “by the Word and Wisdom”
(Wis. 9.1; Ps 103.24), and without this one nothing will be, whereas, according to you, he (i.e. God) does not have anyone in whom and by
whom he makes everything. If then the divine essence itself is not generative, but barren, according to them, as a light that does not light and a dry fountain, how is it that they are not ashamed to say that he has a framing power? 3. And, destroying that which is by nature, how is it that they are not ashamed in their desire to place before (it) what is by will? But if things that are external and previously did not exist are willed by him to be, (and) he makes (them) and becomes their maker, then indeed much more will he be Father of offspring from his own essence.


[p. 428] {#po.56.p428}
рєчєно боуди. аштє хотѣlниѧ имать. и хотѣниє єго сътворєно єсть| и довълєть волѧ єго на състоѧниє бываѭштий. слово жє єго єсть сътворьное · и съдѣтєль бес прѧ ꙗко самъ єсть отьчь живыи съвѣтъ. и въєстьствьно дѣиство. ⁘ и слово истиньно. О нємьжє и състоитьсѧ. и строитъсѧ вьсє добрѣ- τὸ αὐτὸ ῥητέον. Εἰ τὸ βούλεσθαι ἔχει, καὶ τὸ βούλημα αὐτοῦ ποιητικόν ἐστι, καὶ ἀρκεῖ τὸ βούλημα αὐτοῦ πρὸς σύστασιν τῶν γινομένων· ὁ δε Λόγος ἐστὶν αὐτοῦ ποιητικὸς, καὶ δημιουργός· οὐκ ἀμφίβολον, ὅτι αὐτός ἐστιν ἡ τοῦ Πατρὸς ζῶσα βουλὴ, καὶ ἐνούσιος ἐνέργεια, κα Λόγος ἀληθινὸς, ἐν ῷ καὶ συνέστηκε καὶ διοικεῖται τὰ πάντα καλῶς.

4. If then they attribute to God a desire towards the non-existing things, then why do they not acknowledge that which is above the counsel of God? For what surpasses the desire is the fact that it is natural for him to exist and that he is the Father of his own Word. If then what comes first, which is by nature, did not exist, according to their insanity, how will there be what comes second, which is by will? Well, that which comes first is the Word, and second (to it) is the work.

3. 1. So considering the great absurdity that has been pointed out in this Oration with respect to those who say that he is not the Son of God but a work, it is now necessary for us also to confess the same thing that the Lord is Son. But if he is Son, as indeed he is, for it is confessed that the Son is not from without but from the begetter, let them not argue about the terms, as | said before, even if about the Word himself the saints, instead of saying “he begat”, say “he made”, without distinguishing this later term (from the former) in such instances, as long as it is confessed what (he) is by nature. 2. For terms

5. For “Sarah called Abraham lord” (1 Pet 3.6), yet she was not a slave but was a wife. And the apostle to Philemon the purchaser joined the slave Onesimus as a brother (Philem 16). And Bathsheba, while being mother, called her son a slave, saying to the father, “your slave
4. But if they are again bothered because of what is written, “Who was faithful to the one who made him” (Heb 3.2), thinking that the term “faithful” speaks of him as of all others, as if he receives faith and on the ground of that faith he expects a reward, then because of this they also must find fault with Moses for saying, “God faithful and true” (Deut 32.4; Ex 34.6; Num 14.18), and with Paul for writing, “Faithful is God who will not let you receive the temptations beyond what you can bear” (1 Cor 10.13).
5. But in saying this, the saints were not thinking of God in a human way. Rather, they acknowledged a twofold meaning of the word “faithful” in the Scriptures — one as “believing”, and the other as “trustworthy” — the first (as applied) to men, and the second as fitting for God. 6. Abraham then is faithful because he had faith in what God said (Gen 15.6); and God is faithful, as David sings, “God is faithful in his words” (Ps 144.13), and he is trustworthy, and “it is impossible for him to lie” (Heb 6.18).
And “If any faithful (woman) has widows (in her care)” (1 Tim 5.16), she is called faithful because she believes well, but “It is a faithful saying” (1 Tim 3.1; Titus 3.8) because that which it stated must be (so), for it is true and is not otherwise.

7. And what is written, “who was faithful to the one who made him” (Heb 3.2), has no analogy with others, nor (does it mean) that he pleased (God) by his believing. But as being Son of the true God, he too is faithful, (and) must be believed concerning things he says and does, himself remaining unalterable and unchanged (Mal 3.6) in his human economy and in (his) coming in flesh.
7. 1. Thus then everyone who stands against their shamelessness can rebuke them from the single expression “he has made” for straying into error and thinking that God’s Word is a work.

#p# Therefore also with respect to those who seek whether the Lord is a work and creature, it is proper to seek first whether he is Son, and Word, and Wisdom. For if this is shown, the doubt concerning work and creation immediately goes away and ends. 2. For a work will not
καὶ ἐξ αὐτῆς δεῖξαι τῶν αἰρετικῶν τὴν ἀλογίαν, ἐὰν μάλιστα, ὡς ἐν
be Son and Word, nor will the Son be a work and creature.

This being so, again the proof is obvious to all that the phrase, saying “made him” (Heb 3.2) is of no help to their heresy, but rather to (their) shame. 3. For it has been shown that the term “made” (Deut 32.6) is also applied in the divine Scriptures to the genuine and natural children. Therefore, the Lord is shown to be the natural and genuine Son, Word, and Wisdom of the Father; and even if “he (was) made” is said of him, or “became,” this is not said of him as if he were a work, but the saints use these terms in different ways, as in the case with Solomon and with Hezekiah’s children.
2. But now, since the meaning of what is written is straight, showing when and to whom the word “he has made’ is said, it is necessary from this also to show the irrationality of the heretics, especially if, as was said before, we take into account the time and need (for which it was written). The apostle then is not talking about (the time) before things were made when he said this, but when “the Word became flesh” (Jn 1.14), for this is what is written: “Therefore, holy brothers,
partakers of the heavenly calling, look at the apostle and high-priest of our profession, Jesus, who was faithful to the one who made him” (Heb 3.1-2).

4. For also (regarding) those who had begotten (them) from themselves, it is written, “made”, and “obtained”, and “became”. Therefore, God-fighters, who repeatedly put forth such terms, must, though at some later point, after what has been said, reject the impious thinking and think of the Lord as the true Son, Word, and Wisdom of the Father, not a work, nor a creature.
3. But when was he sent, if not when he clothed himself in our flesh? And when did he become “the high-priest of our profession” (Heb 3.1), if not when he offered himself for us, raised (his) body from the dead, and now he himself brings and offers to the Father those who come to him in faith, redeeming all and praying to God for all?
4. Is it not then because he wished to express the essence of the Word or the natural generation from the Father, that the apostle said, “who was faithful to the one who made him” (Heb 3.2)? In no way! For the Word is maker, but the Word is not made. But it is his descent to mankind and the high-priesthood that did “become”, which is well (seen), if one looks at the history of the Law and Aaron.

In the same way then also the Lord, “In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God” (Jn 1.1). He writes then in the epistle to the Hebrews, “But as much then as the children share flesh and blood, he too closely partook of them, so that by death he might destroy the one who holds the power of death, that is, the devil, and forgive those who through fear of death were during their entire life subject to death. 6. For it is not (the nature of) angels that he took on himself, but it is the seed of Abraham that he took on himself. Therefore he had to be made alike in everything to the brothers, so that he might be a merciful and faithful high-priest to cleanse the sins of people. For in that he himself suffered being tempted, he can help those who are being assaulted. Therefore, holy brothers, the partakers of a heavenly calling, look at the apostle and high-priest of our confession, Jesus, faithful to the one who made him” (Heb 2.14-3.2).

2. Therefore, Paul was writing about the human economy of the Word, when he said, “who was faithful to the one who made him” (Heb 3.2), and not about his essence. So do not be mad anymore, saying that the Word is a work, 3. for he is Son, by nature the only-begotten, but then he had brothers when he clothed himself in flesh like ours and which he by himself offered. He was named high-priest and became “merciful and faithful” (Heb 2.17) — merciful because he offered himself for us by having mercy on us, whereas faithful, not as sharing (our) faith, nor as believing in anyone as we do, but as the one who must be believed about that which he says and does, and as offering a faithful sacrifice, one that remains and does not fail.

10. 1. As for the so-called gods of the Greeks, falsely named so, they are neither faithful with respect to being, nor with respect to promise, nor are they everywhere the same. But, being in (a certain) place and time, they perish and disappear in themselves. Therefore, the Word cries out against them that “faith does not endure” (Jer 9.2) in them, and they are (like) “false water” (Jer 15.18), and “there is no faith in them” (Deut 32.20). But the God of all, being truly one and true, is faithful. Being (always) the same, he says, “See me, see me, that | am (he)” (Deut 32.39) and “do not change” (Mal 3.6).

3. Therefore, indeed, the apostle, telling of the bodily coming of the Word, said an “apostle”, and “faithful to the one who made him” (Heb 3.1-2), showing that even when made man, “Jesus Christ is the same yesterday, and today, and forever” (Heb 13.8) unchanging. Thus, as the apostle reminds us of his being made man when he writes about (his) high-priesthood, so he does not keep silence again about his divinity, but immediately mentions (it), providing (us with) firmness in all respects, especially when he calls him “humbled”, so that we may immediately recognize his loftiness and fatherly majesty.

11. 1. If it has been shown that even when someone says “he has made” with regard to the Word himself, he (actually) says “begat”, then what (further) notions concerning this can they invent? For having cleared up the word in every respect, the (present) discussion shows that the Son is not a work, but the Father's offspring by nature, while in economy by the Father's good pleasure, he was made for our sake and is constituted as man.

5. And men who are acting and subsist are clothed in flesh, but the Word of God became man in order to sanctify flesh, and while being Lord, he was in the image of the slave (Phil 2.7), for the entire work is the slave of the Word, as it came to be and was made by him. #p# 6. From this it holds that the phrase “he has made”, spoken by the apostle, shows not that the Word is made, but that he assumed a body like ours and thus became a brother to us (Jn 20.17; Heb 2.11-12), having become man.

For all these words are proper to human constitution, and as such they reveal not the essence of the Word, but (the fact) that he became man. That is the meaning of the expression in Acts, which they bring up, (where) Peter says that “he has made him both Lord and Christ, that same Jesus whom you crucified” (Acts 2.36). For here too it is not written, “he has made for himself a Son”, or “he has made for himself a Word” that they should think in such a way.

2. For Peter, having said, “He has made him Lord and Christ’, immediately added by saying, “this Jesus whom you crucified” (Acts 2.36). And to all it became obvious, and let it be so to them as well, if they are to remain consistent, that not the essence of the Word, but he according to (his) humanity is said to have been made. For what was crucified, if not the body? And how would he indicate the bodily condition of the Word, except by saying, “he made”?

4. (So) then it is about his becoming in this way Lord and king that Peter says, “he has made him Lord” (Acts 2.36) and “has sent Christ” (Acts 3.20). This is precisely the same as to say that the Father made him man, for it is proper to man to be made. He has not simply made him man, but has made him to have power over all and to sanctify all with the anointment.

7. Let then no one doubt anymore, but (recognize that) ‘the whole house of Israel knows firmly that this Jesus’ (Acts 2.36), whom you saw ‘in the image of a man’ (Phil 2.7), doing signs and such works as no one has ever done, he himself is Christ and Lord of all. 8. For though made man, and ‘named Jesus’ (Lk 2.21), as we previously said, he was not diminished by human passion, but rather, even being made man, he is manifested as Lord of the living and the dead. ‘For since’, as the apostle said, ‘in the wisdom of God the world did not recognize God by wisdom, God was pleased by the foolishness of preaching to save those who believe’ (1 Cor 1.21).

3. And the fact that this (statement) alone, said by Peter, (namely), “He has made him Lord and Christ” (Acts 2.36), does not show the Son to be work, can be seen from the blessing of Isaac, even though this image is dark as regards our subject. Now, he said to Jacob, “Be lord over your brother” (Gen 27.29), and to Esau, “Behold, | made him your Lord” (Gen 27.37). Now, even though the word “made” shows the beginning of Jacob’s essence and existence, in such case too they must not think such things about the Word of God. For the Son of God is not a work as Jacob. On the contrary, they could somehow learn by asking questions, and not be insane on this point.

“| made’, indicating neither the beginning of Jacob’s existence then, nor his essence — for after thirty years and more of his existence, he says this —, but the authority over his brother which came later.

Yet as one who gives the Spirit, the same one is also said to be made among us as Lord on account of (his) humanity, while giving (us the Spirit) because he is God’s Word. 3. If then, according to you, he is just a creature, how is it that you act as hypocrites, saying, “but not as one of the creatures”? And if he is just a work, how is he “not as one of the works”? In this we can see the poison of the heresy. For by saying, “offspring, but not as one of the offspring”, they place many sons in the same rank, and one of these they declare to be the Lord, so that according to them he is no more only-begotten, but one out of many brothers, and is [+ called gr.] offspring and son.

4. What need then is there in this pretense of saying that he is a creature and saying that he is not a creature? For though you say, “not as one of the creatures”, such scheming thinking will appear to be nonsense. For again, you declare him to be one of the creatures, and whatever someone says of the other creatures, such you also imagine with regard to the Son, (acting) truly as “fools and blind” (Mt 23.17). For which of the creatures is just as another (creature) that you should say this about the Son as something exceptional?

2. For though the Son is superior to the others by comparison, yet he is in no way lesser (than them), a creature as they are, because in those who are by nature works it can be found that they excel one another. For “one star differs from another star in glory” (1 Cor 15.41), and the rest (of the creatures), when compared to each other, have their own differences. But it is not because of this that some are above, while others serve the superior, nor that some are causes of works, while others by them come into being, but all have a nature that comes to be and is created, confessing in themselves their framer, as David sings, “The heavens profess the glory of God, and the firmament declares the work of his hand” (Ps 18.2), and as Zerubbabel the wise says, “All the earth calls upon the truth and heaven blesses it, and all works shake and tremble” (1 Esd 4.36).

22. 1. But if God also does and forms out of existing material — which is a Greek thought, according to which God is called an artificer and not a maker —, then let the Word in that way work on the material at the command and in the service of God. But if he calls into existence things that did not exist, (and that) by his own Word, (then) the Word is not from things non-existing and called, and let us not seek another Word, by whom he too was called, for it is by the Word that non-existing things come to be.
how does he ever alone reveal the Father and no one but he know the Father (Jn 10.15)?
4. For if it is possible for a work to know the Father, then he must be known by all according to each one’s measure, for all are works as he himself is. But if it is impossible for things originate either to see or know, for the vision and mind that is in him surpasses all — since God himself said, “No one will see my face and live” (Ex 33.20) —, yet the Son said, “No one knows the Father but the Son” (Mt 11.27), then the Word is different from things generated, alone knowing and alone seeing the Father, as he said, “Not that anyone has seen the Father but the one who is from the Father” (Jn 6.46), and “No one knows the Father but the Son” (Mt 11.27), even though Arius does not want this to be the case.

It would have been more proper for them to say this while covering (their face) in order to not be greatly shamed. γίνεσθαι. Τοῦτο δὲ λέγειν αὐτοὺς ἐγκαλυπτομένους ἔπρεπεν, ἵνα μὴ πολλὴν αἰσχύνην ἀπενέγκωνται. Μωϋσῆς γὰρ οὐ δημιουργεῖν ἐπέμπετο, οὐδὲ τὰ μὴ ὅντα εἰς τὸ εῖναι καλέσαι, καὶ πλάσαι τοὺς ὁμοίους ἀνθρώπους, ἀλλὰ διακονῆσαι μόνον ῥήματα πρὸς τὸν λαὸν
with words to the people, and to king Pharaoh. And this has a big difference, because to minister belongs to things originate as slaves, but to create and to frame belongs to God alone, and to his proper Word and Wisdom. 2. Thus, as far as the (making of) the work is concerned, we will find no one else but God’s Word, for “everything comes to be by Wisdom” (Ps 103.24), and “without the Word nothing came to be” (Jn 1.3).
3. How full is then that folly, (according to which) what already came into being, they think to be impossible of coming to be? Or perhaps they deceive (by claiming) that they did not even come to be, (as) still seeking their mediator, for according to their insane and impious reason, the originate things would not have been made without the mediator.

creatures and only one Word, who will not figure out from this that the Son differs from all and has no equality with the creatures, but is proper to the Father? Thus, there are not many Words, but only one Word of the one Father and one Image of the one God. “But behold,” they say, “the sun is the sole one too, and the earth is one”. 6. Insane men, let them also say that water is one and fire is one. Let them hear that each of the originate things is one according to its own essence, yet with respect to the ministry and service entrusted to it, no one by itself is adequate nor sufficient alone. For God also said, “Let there be lights in the heavenly firmament to give light upon the earth and to divide between the day and between the night; and let them be for signs, and for seasons, and for days, and for years” (Gen 1.14).

2. In the same way also the earth is not for all things but just for the fruits and (to be a place) to walk on for the living animals that are from it. “The firmament is to divide between waters and waters” (Gen 1.7), and the stars are set on it. Likewise also fire and water with all the rest have been made to constitute the bodies, and on the whole no single thing is alone, but each of the existing things — like “members to each other as if one body” (Rom 12.4-5) — complete this beauty

yet as if from a teacher [+ and gr.] maker he learned to frame, and he has ministered to God that taught him’. For this also Asterius the Sophist, having learned to deny the Lord, has dared to write despite the absurdity that follows from it. For if framing is a thing to be taught, let them be cautious, lest they say that God himself is a framer not by nature but by acquired learning, which therefore can also depart from him.
5. Then if the Wisdom of God attained (the ability) to frame by learning, how is he still Wisdom when he requires learning? And what was he before he learned? For it was not Wisdom, if it required teaching. Indeed, it was some empty thing and not essential Wisdom, but from advancement it received the name of Wisdom, and will be Wisdom only as long as it keeps what it has learned. For what one does not have by nature, but acquired by learning, that thing can at a point in time be also unlearned. But to say this about the Word of God befits not Christians, but the Greeks.

30. 1. First, the Son appears rather to have been brought to be for our sake, and not we for his sake. [+ For we were not gr.] created [+ for him gr], but he was made for our sake, [+ so gr.] he owes thanks to us and not we to him, as the woman to the man. “For the man’, the Scriptures say, “was not created for the woman's sake, but the woman for the man’s sake” (1 Cor 11.9). Thus then as the man “is the image and glory of God, whereas the woman is the glory of the man” (1 Cor 11.7), so are we also God's image and are made to exist for his glory, but the Son is our image and was made for our glory.

3. But if this is the case, it may be that the Father has not wanted the Son at all, for he has created him unwillingly, whereas us he created having a desire (to do so). He has made him for our sake, for he designed him after he (had designed) us. Hence, according to these impious men, the Son is now superfluous, being brought to be as an instrument; it is for the sake of originate things that he was created. 4. But if the Son, as alone capable of enduring it, came to be by God alone, while we, as incapable of enduring it, came to be by the Word, why does he not first take counsel about him, as one who is capable of enduring his making, but rather about us? Or why does he not prefer the strong over the weak? Or when making him first, why does he not take counsel about him first? Or why taking counsel about us first, did he not make us first, given that his will is sufficient for the constitution of originate things? But he creates him first, whereas he counsels first about us; and he wills us prior to the Mediator, and when he wills to create us and counsels about us, he calls us creatures, whereas him, whom he makes for our sake, he calls Son, and his proper one, and heir.

2. For if it was for the glory of God not to make the things originate still had the Word been no less “with God” (Jn 1.1) and the Father been in him. But things originate could not come to be without the Word. Hence they were made by him — to say the right thing. For since the Word is the Son of God by generation proper to his essence, (and) is from him, and “in him” (Jn 10.38), as he said himself, the things framed could not have come to be except through him.

32. 1. From this then it is seen that the Arians are not fighting with us about their heresy, but while they play a trick with us, they actually fight against the Godhead itself. For if the voice were ours that says, “This is my Son” (Mt 3.17), small would be the complaint from us
7. For each of these has the mediating Word and the Wisdom of God that communicates the will of the Father. But when the Word himself frames and creates, in that instance there is neither questioning nor answer, for the Father is in him and the Word in the Father (Jn 10.38). But the will is sufficient for the work to be done, so that the phrase “he said” (Gen 1.6), (as pronounced) by the Father is a recognizable sign of the will for our sake, and the phrase “it was so” (Gen 1.6) points to the work that is done through the Word and Wisdom, in which also is the will of the Father. And the phrase “God said” is recognized in the Word, for he said, “You have made all things by Wisdom” (Ps 103.24): and “By the Word of the Lord the heavens were made firm” (Ps 32.6); and “There is one Lord Jesus Christ by whom is everything, and we are by him” (1 Cor 8.6).

4. And as for work, it is enough for discerning that there is a God and a providence — “for by the greatness of beauty, and [from gr.] the creation of the world, the framer is equally revealed” (Wis 13.5) —, and we learn from them without asking for voices, but hearing from Scriptures, we take faith, and seeing the very order and the harmony of everything, we acknowledge his existence — that he is maker, and master, and God of all —, and apprehend his marvelous providence and lordship over everything. In the same manner about the divinity of the Son, what was said before is sufficient, and it becomes superfluous, or rather it is complete madness to dispute or to inquire about it heretically: How can the Son be eternal? Or how can he be from the Father's essence, yet not part (of it)? For what is said to be of another is not part of him, and what is divided is no longer whole.

3. And from this we also consider the Son as being not from outside, but as begotten from the Father. And with the Father being whole, “the expression of (his) hypostasis” (Heb 1.3) always exists and has the Father's likeness and unchanged image, so anyone who sees him, sees in him the hypostasis too, of which he is the expression. From the activity of the expression, we apprehend the true divinity of the hypostasis. This the Savior himself points out by saying, “The Father who abides in me, he does the deeds” (Jn 14.10) which | do (Jn 10.25),

4. Therefore, let this Christ-fighting heresy attempt first to divide the stumbling instances related to the existing things and say whether the sun was once without radiance, or that radiance is not proper to the essence of light, or (even if it is) proper, it is a part of light by division. And again, let it divide the Word and say that it is foreign to mind, or that once it was not, or that it is not proper to its essence, or that it is by division a part of that (mind). As regards the expression, the light, and the power, let it divide (these) in the (same) way as it did the Word and the light, and then imagine (its) rubbish if it wants.

3. But the human word is composed of syllables, so that it is neither alive, nor effects (anything), but is only a signification of the mind of the speaker and only goes out and passes by, not to appear again, because it was not at all before it was spoken. Therefore, the human word is not alive, nor effects (anything), nor at all is man. It undergoes this, as | said before, because man who begets it has a nature that is out of nothing. But God’s Word is not (merely) uttered forth, as someone might say, nor (is it) a sound of speech, nor is his Son God's command, but as radiance of light, so is he perfect offspring from the perfect one. 4. Therefore, God's image is also God, for it is said, “The Word was God” (Jn 1.1). And human words are of no good for causing an effect. Hence one does things not with words, but with human hands, as these have being, whereas a word does not subsist by itself.


[p. 557] {#po.56.p557}
чловѣчьское слово. поⷧємлєть же то ꙗкоⷤⷧ прѣⷣрєкохъ. ꙁанеⷤ и раждаѧ нє чловѣкъ отъ неісѫштааго иматъ сѫтьство · божие жє слово, | не ꙗкоⷤ кто рєчеⷮ, иꙁносимо єсть, ни тѫтьнъ глаіголъ
5. “The Word of God”, as the apostle said, “is living and active, and sharper than any double-edged sword, and piercing even to dividing soul and spirit, joints and marrow, and is discerning the intentions and thoughts of the heart. And there is no creature that is not visible before him. Everything is naked and open before the eyes of him to whom is our account” (Heb 4.12-13). He is then active, and “without him nothing came to be” (Jn 1.3), nor can anything come to be without him.

Ὁ δὲ τοῦ Θεοῦ Λόγος, ὡς εἷπεν ὁ Ἀπόστολος, ζῶν ἐστιν ὁ Λόγος τοῦ Θεοῦ καὶ ἐνεργὴς, καὶ τομώτερος ὑπὲρ πᾶσαν μάχαιραν δίστομον, και διικνούμενος ἅχρι μερισμοῦ ψυχῆς καὶ πνεύματος, ἀρμῶν τε καὶ μυελῶν, καὶ κριτικὸς ἐνθομήσεων καὶ ἐννοιῶν καρδίας· καὶ οὐκ ἔστι κτίσις ἀφανὴς ἐνώπιον αὐτοῦ, πάντα δὲ γομνὰ καὶ τετραχηλισμένα τοῖς ὀφθαλμοῖς αὐτοῦ, πρὸς ὁ͂ν ἥμῖν ὁ λόγος. Δημιουργὸς οῦν ἐστι, καὶ χωρὶς αὐτοῦ ἐγένετο ούδὲ ἑ͂ν, οὐδὲ δυνατόν τι γίνεσθαι χωρὶς αὐτοῦ.

Οὺ δεῖ δὲ ζητεῖν, διὰ τί μὴ τοιοῦτος ὁ τοῦ Θεοῦ Λόγος οἶος και ὁ ἡμέτερος· ἐπεὶ μὴ τοιοῦτος ὁ Θεὸς οἶοι καὶ ἡμεῖς, ὡς προείρηται ἀλλ᾽ οὐδὲ πρέπει ζητεῖν πῶς ἐκ τοῦ Θεοῦ ἐστιν ὁ Λόγος, ἣ πῶος ἀπαύγασμά ἐστι τοῦ Θεοῦ, ἣ πῶς γεννᾷ ὁ Θεὸς, καὶ τίς ὁ τρόπος τῆς τοῦ Θεοῦ γεννήσεως·
36. 1. We must not seek why God's Word is not such as ours, nor is it fitting to seek in what way the Word is from God, or in what way he is the radiance of God, or how God begets and what manner of God's begetting is.

Ἀλλ᾽ ὥσπερ τὸ τοιοῦτο ἐρωτᾶν ἀσεβές ἐστι, καὶ ἀγνοούντων τὸν Θεὸν, οὕτω καὶ οὐ θέμις οὐδὲ περὶ τῆς τοῦ Υἵοῦ τοῦ Θεοῦ γεννήσεως τοιαῦτα τολμᾶν οὐδὲ τῇ ἑαυτῶν φύσει καὶ ἀσθενείᾳ συμμετρεῖν τὸν Θεὸν καὶ τὴν τούτου σοφίαν·
2. One must be frantic (if) one dares to (fathom) such issues, since a thing ineffable and proper to the nature of God, known to him alone and to the Son, this he demands to be explained in words. It is exactly the same as seeking (to answer) where God is, and how God is, and (of what nature) the Father is. But just as to ask such (questions) is misbelieving and (typical) of those who do not know God, so is it not fitting to venture (asking) about the generation of the Son of God, nor relate God and his Wisdom by means of our own nature and weakness.
нъ ни сєгоⷤ ради подобаеть и
3. Nor does it befit on this account to think of anything else besides


[p. 560] {#po.56.p560}
кромѣ истины раꙁꙋмѣвати. ни достоить же аште кто и нєдооумѣєть иштѧ о сиⷯ. нє вѣровати писанъимъ. оунє бо єсть недооумѣѭштемꙑ млъчѧти и вѣровати. нежє не вѣровати ꙁа нєдооумѣниѧ. ꙗко недооумѣѧи. понѣ отърадѫ имать. ꙗко отънѫдь понѣ искавъ оуімлъчалъ єсть. Онъ же рєкъши нє недооумѣѭ. примышлѧѭть сєбѣ нє подобнаѧ, и недо-* -стоинаѧ о боꙁѣ вѣштаваѧ, неотъпѫштакемь сѫдъ съмѣниѧ имать. ἀλλ᾽ οὐδὲ διὰ τοῦτο καὶ παρὰ τὴν ἀλήθειαν νοεῖν προσήκει· οὐδὲ εἰ ἀπορεῖ τις ζητῶν περὶ τούτων, ὀφείλει καὶ ἀπιστεῖν τοῖς γεγραμμένοις Βέλτιον γὰρ ἀποροῦντας σιωπᾶν καὶ πιστεύειν, ἣ ἀπιστεῖν διὰ τὸ ἀπορεῖν· διότι ὁ μὲν ἀπορῶν δύναταί πως καὶ συγγνώμην ἔχειν, ὅτι ὅλως κᾶ̀ν ζητήσας ἠρέμησεν· ὁ δὲ διὰ τὸ ἀπορεῖν ἐπινοῶν έαυτῷ τὸ μὴ δέοντα, καὶ τὰ μὴ ἅξια περὶ Θεοῦ φθεγγόμενος, ἀσύγγνωστον ἕχε τῆς τόλμης τὴν δίκην.
the truth. Nor does anyone, if perplexed while inquiring about these things, have to disbelieve what is written. For it is better for the perplexed to be silent and believe, than not to believe because of the perplexity, for one who is perplexed may in some way be pardoned, because though he has sought, he remained completely silent. But the one who says that he is perplexed and forms for himself unseemly ideas and proclaims unworthy things about God, he will have an unforgiving verdict (in view of such) doubting [audacity gr.].


[p. 561] {#po.56.p561}
можєть бо и такоівыиⷯ нєдооумѣнии. нѣкакѫ оутѣхоу имѣти отъ божиѝ книгъ. ꙗко приимати добрѣ написаниѧ. раꙁоумѣвати жє ꙗкоⷤ притчею наlшє слово. ꙗко, ꙗко сє свое отъ насъ єсть, и не кромѣ наⷭ҇ дѣло. такождє и б҃жие слово. своє есть отъ него. и нѣсть сътворениє. ни ꙗкоⷤ чловѣчьское слово. аштє ли то и б҃а҃ нѫжда єсты раꙁоумѣвати чловѣка. Δύναται γὰρ καὶ τῶν τοιούτων ἀποριῶν ἔχειν τινὰ παραμυθίαν ἐκ τῶν θείων Γραφῶν, ὥστε λαμβάνειν μὲν καλῶς τὰ γεγραμμένα, νοεῖν δὲ ὡς ἐν παραδείγματι τὸν ἡμέτερον λόγον· ὅτι ὥσπερ οὗτος ἱδιος ἐξ ἡμῶν ἐστι, καὶ οὐκ ἔξωθεν ἡμῶν ἔργον, οὕτω καὶ ὁ τοῦ Θεοῦ Λόγος ἵδιός ἐστιν ἐξ αὐτοῦ, καὶ οὐκ ἔστι ποίημα, οὐδὲ ὡς ὁ τῶν ἀνθρώπων λόγος· ἐπεὶ καὶ τὸν Θεὸν ἀνάγκη νοεῖν ἅνθρωπον.
4. For in such perplexities he can have some relief from the divine Scriptures, when he takes rightly what is written and thinks about our word as an illustration, (recognizing) that as it is proper to us (and) is from us, and not a work external to us, so also God's Word is proper (to him and) from him, and is not a work, nor like the human word, or else we need to understand God to be man.
єсє бо пакы чл҃чьскаѧ словєса. мнѡга и раꙁличьна. по всѧ д҃ни исходѧⷮ. ꙁанеⷩⷤ прьваѧ нє прєбываѭть. нъ расыпаѭтсѧ то бываєть, ꙁанеⷤ и отьци ихъ чловѣци сѫштє. мимоходѧшта имѫть раштенїа мыслиⷧ находѧштѧ. и ꙗкоⷤ и мыслѧть. такождє и вѣштаѭть. тѣмꙿжє и мнѡга слоівєса имѫть нъ ни едино Ἰδοὺ γὰρ πάλιν τῶν μὲν ἀνθρώπων πολλοὶ καὶ διάφοροι λόγοι καθʼ ἡμέραν παρέρχονται, διὰ τὸ τοὺς πρώτους μὴ μένειν, ἀλλ’ ἀφανίζεσθαι. Γίνεται δὲ πάλιν τοῦτο, ἐπεὶ καὶ οἱ τούτων πατέρες, ἅνθρωποι ὅντες, παρερχομένας μὲν ἔχουσι τὰς ἡλικίας, τὰ δὲ νοήματα ἐπερχόμενα· καὶ πρὸς ἃ λογίζονται καὶ ἐπιλογίζονται, τοιαῦτα καὶ φθέγγονται· ὥστε καὶ πολλοὺς λόγους ἔχειν, καὶ μετὰ τοὺς πολλοὺς

also they have many words, but of them not a single one remains. For (once) the speaker stops, the word also immediately dissolves.

6. But the Word of God is one and the same, as (the psalmist) writes, “The Word of God remains for ages” (Ps 118.89), unchanged (Mal 3.6), not (as) first or second after another, but existing always the same. For given that God is one, it befits that there would also be one image, and one Word, and one Wisdom.

ἐ̔́μφυτον αὐτῷ καὶ συνυπάρχουσαν αὐτῷ ἀγεννήτως κηρύσσων, γεννητικὴν μὲν οὖσαν δηλονότι τοῦ Χριστοῦ, δημιουργικὴν δὲ τοῦ παντὸς κόσμου, περὶ ἦς ἐν τῆ πρὸς Ῥωμαίους Ἐπιστολῇ διδάσκωυν λέγει· Τὰ γὰρ ἀόρατα αυτοῦ ἀπὸ κτίσεως κόσμου τοῖς ποιήμασι νοούμενα καθορᾶται, ἥ τε ἀίδιος αύτοῦ δύναμις καὶ θειότης. "Ὡσπερ γὰρ τὴν εἰρημένην ἐνταῦθα θειότητα οὐκ ἄν τις φαίη Χριστὸν εἷναι ἀλλ᾽ αὐτὸν ὑπάρχειν τὸν Πατέρα· οὕτως οἷμαι καὶ ἡ αΐδιος αὐτοῦ δύναμις καὶ θειότης, οὐχ ὁ Μονογενὴς Υἱὸς, ἀλλ᾽ ὁ γεννήσας ὑπάρχε Πατήρ. Ἄλλην δὲ δύναμιν καὶ σοφίαν διδάσκει Θεοῦ εῖναι διὸ Χριστοῦ δεικνυμένην.»
(1 Cor 1.24). He preached that there was another power proper to God himself that coexisted in him ingenerately. (This power), apparently, generates Christ and creates the entire world, concerning which he teaches in his epistle to the Romans, saying, ‘For the invisible things of him are understood by means of works that are visible, even his eternal power and Godhead’ (Rom 1.20). No one would say that the Godhead spoken of here is Christ, but the Father himself, so | think that his eternal power and Godhead also is not the only-begotten Son, but the Father who begot him, and he teaches another power and Wisdom of God, revealed through Christ”.

4. And again, how is it not an extreme folly of their thinking to say that the ingenerate [unoriginate gr.] Wisdom coexisting with God is God himself? For that which coexists with another, does not coexist with itself, as the evangelists say of the Lord that he was together with the disciples (Lk 9.18). For he was not together with himself but with the disciples, unless perhaps they would say that God is compound, having wisdom as a constituent or completing (part) of his essence, unoriginate as well as himself, which they introduce as that which framed the world, so that they might deprive the Son of (his) framing (activity). For they are forced to say anything to not just think about the Lord according to the truth.

4. For it is their invention and Christ-fighting (ideas). They keep the name “Word” and “Wisdom” but make for themselves others, denying the genuine Word of God and the real [and gr.] only Wisdom of God. And (these) damned men imitate the Manichees, for they too, when seeing the works of God, deny him, the only real [and gr.] genuine God, and design for themselves another, whom they can show neither by work, nor in any testimony drawn from the Scriptures.

2. Therefore, the Jews, together with these ones (i.e. the Arians), when “denying the Son, do not have the Father either” (1 Jn 2.23). Having left the “Fountain of Wisdom” (Bar 3.12), as Baruch said reproaching them, they put away from them the existing Wisdom, our Lord Jesus Christ — for Christ, said the apostle, is “God’s power and God's Wisdom’ (1 Cor 1.24) —, when they said, “We have no king but Caesar” (Jn 19.15). For the Jews bear the punishment for (their) denial — because their city, as well as their reasoning, perished. But as for these ones (i.e. the Arians), they take the risk of (loosing) the fullness of the mystery itself, that is, baptism.

Οὐ γὰρ ὁ λέγων ἀπλῶς, Κύριε, οὗτος καὶ δίδωσιν· ἀλλʼ ὁ μετὰ τοῦ ὀνόματος καὶ τὴν πίστιν ἔχων ὀρθήν. Διὰ τοῦτο γοῦν καὶ ὁ Σωτὴρ οὐγ ἀπλῶς ἐνετείλατο βαπτίζειν, ἀλλὰ πρῶτόν φησι, Μαθητεύσατε εἶθ ούτω· Βαπτίζετε εις ὅνομα Πατρὸς, καὶ Yίοῦ, καὶ ἀγίου Πνεύματος ΐν ἐκ τῆς μαθήσεως ἡ πίστις ὀρθῆ̀ γένηται, καὶ μετὰ πίστεως ἡ τοιῦ βαπτίσματος τελείωσις προστεθῆ
4. For just as the creature is other than the Son, in the same way (the baptism) falsely administered by them is other than the truth. And they use the name Father and Son hypocritically, just because (they) are written (in Scriptures) (Mt 28.19), for it is not he who simply says, “Lord” (Mt 7.21), gives baptism, but he who with the name also has the simple faith. For this reason, the Savior also did not simply command to baptize, but first said, “Teach” (and) then, “Baptize in the name of the Father, and Son, and Holy Spirit” (Mt 10 28.19), so that the right faith would follow from learning and, together with faith, might be added the consecration of baptism.

disciples of the Samosatene, (though) pronouncing the names, they are nonetheless heretics. In the same way, it also follows that those who hold the Arian (teaching), (though they) respect what is written and pronounce the names, yet they too insult those who receive (baptism) from them, being much more impious than the other heretics and “in some way exceeding them” (Ezek 16.47); they even justify them by the excess of their speech. 3. For these (other heresies all) lie greatly against the truth in one way or another, either committing an error about the body and saying that the Lord did not have flesh from Mary, or (believing) that he was not mortal at all, nor actually became man, but only appeared and was not so in truth — (thus) he seemed to have a body but did not (actually) have it; and he seemed to have the appearance of man as visions in a dream. But these (i.e. the Arians) openly do impious things against the Father himself.


[p. 588] {#po.56.p588}
подъ поръ паіданиѭ єрєси. чловѣчьскѫѭ помошть подълагаѭть. да тоу видѧ. ижє єсты грѫбъ, или и боѧсѧ, да нє съмотрѧеть пакоістьное ꙁломышлєнїѧ ихъ· Како оубо нѣсть достоино миловати прѣльштаємыиⷯ ими. или како нѣсть добро плакатисѧ о сиⷯ. ꙗко тꙑгдадⷩⷧе сластиоу привидѣниоу даѭть своѭ полꙁꙋ. и отъпадаѭть отъ бѫдѫштѧѧ надеждѧ ὥσπερ ἔρεισμα τῷ πτώματι τῆς αἱρέσεως, τὴν ἀνθρωπίνην προστασίαν ὑποτιθέασι, ΐνα ταύτην βλέπων ὁ ἁπλούστερος, ἣ καὶ δεδιὼς, μὴ κατανοῆ τὸ βλαβερὸν τῆς κακοφροσύνης αὐτῶν. Πῶς οὗν οὐκ ἅξιον οἰκτείρειν τοὺς παρ’ αὐτῶν ἀπατωμένους; Ἡ πῶς οὐκ ἐπὶ τούτοις δακρῦσαι καλὸν, ὅτι τῇ παραυτίκα διὰ τὰς ἡδονὰς φαντασίᾳ προδιδόας τὸ ἐαυτοῖς συμφέρον, καὶ ἐκπίπτουσι τῆς μελλούσης ἐλπίδος;
4. For hearing the testimony of Scriptures that his divinity is found in the Son as in the image, they blaspheme, saying that it is a creature, and they carry everywhere the phrase “he was not” regarding him as mud in a sack, and offer it as venom of the serpents. 5. Now, since their teaching is erratic (nauseous gr.), (as is clear) to all, immediately
For in thinking that they take (baptism into the name of) one who does not exist, they will receive nothing; and classing themselves with a creature, they will receive no help from the creation; 6. and believing in one who is unlike and different from the Father's essence, they will not be united with the Father without having his own Son who is by nature from him — who is in the Father and in whom the Father is also (Jn 14.10), as he said himself. But being led astray by them, they, reckless men, end up being devoid and stripped of the Godhead. For this earthly vision (of goods) will not follow them after they die, nor when they see the Lord whom they denied, (and) who sits on the throne of his Father and judges the living and the dead, will they be able to call for help from anyone of those who have now deceived them. For they will see them being judged as well, repenting about their insults and impiety.

as a support against the fall of (their) heresy, they set forth human help, so that the guileless, when seeing it or even fearing it, may not notice their mean evil-thinking. 2. For it is written, “The Lord created me a beginning of his ways for his works” (Prov 8.22), but since these are proverbs, and it is said proverbially, we must not understand an ordinary saying that simply, but seek the person and thus with piety give them meaning. For what is said in the Proverbs is not said explicitly, but is told latently, as the Lord himself has taught (us) in the Gospel according to John, “These things | spoke to you in proverbs; the time will come when | will no longer speak to you in proverbs but openly” (Jn 16.25). Therefore, it is necessary to unfold the meaning of words, seeking it as something hidden, and not understand it as spoken explicitly and simply, lest by misinterpretation we go astray from the truth.

4. (God's Wisdom) does not rank itself among the creatures as if forgetting that it is creator and framer, or (as if) being ignorant of the divide between the creator and the creatures. But it points to a certain meaning, just as in proverbs — not explicitly but latently, which it (i.e. God's Wisdom) inspired the saints to say in their prophecy, while soon after, it shows the (meaning) of “he has created” by other expressions, saying, “Wisdom made itself a house” (Prov 9.1).

Καὶ ταύτην τὴν διαφορὰν οἱ̓͂δεν ἡ θεία Γ̔ραφὴ περὶ μὲν τῶν κτισμάτων λέγουσα· Ἐπληρώθη ἡ γῆ τῆς κτίσεώς σου· καί· Αὐτὴ η κτίσις συστενάζει καὶ συνωδίνει· ἐν δὲ τῆ Ἀποκαλύψει φησί· Καιι ἀπέθανε τὸ τρίτον μέρος τῶν κτισμάτων τῶν ἐν τῇ θαλάσση, τὰ ἔχοντο ψοχάς· καθὼς καὶ ὁ Παῦλος λέγει· Πᾶν κτίσμα Θεοῦ καλὸν, καὶ οὐδὲν ἀπόβλητον μετὰ εύχαριστίας λαμβανόμενον· ἐν δὲ τῆ Σοφία γέγραπται Καὶ τῆ σοφίᾳ σου κατεσκεόασας τὸν ἄνθρωπον, ίνα δεσπόζη τῶν ὑπὸ σοῦ γενομένων κτισμάτων
2. For the creatures, having a created essence, are of originate things and are said to be created, and (indeed) every creature is created. But the expression that says, “he has created”, does not at all disclose the essence or the generation, but shows something else as coming to pass in him of whom it speaks. And not every being that is said to be created is by nature a creature; and this difference the divine Scriptures recognize as they say about the creatures: “The earth is full of your creation” (Ps 103.24); and “the creation itself groans together and agonizes together” (Rom 8.22); and in the Revelation it is said, “And the third part of the sea creatures that had soul (i.e. life) died” (Rev 8.9): as also Paul says, “Every creature of God is good, and nothing is to be rejected if it is received with thanksgiving” (1 Tim 4.4); and in (the Book of) Wisdom it is written, “And you made man by your Wisdom, that he should have dominion over the creatures made to exist by you” (Wis 9.2).

sin for us” (2 Cor 5.21), we do not conceive that he entirely became curse and sin, but that he took the curse that was against us, as the apostle said, “Having redeemed us from the curse” (Gal 3.13), and our sins, as Isaiah said, “He has borne” (Isa 53.4; Mt 8.17), whereas Peter said, “He bore them up in the body on the wood” (1 Pet 2.24). Likewise, if we hear in the Proverbs, “he has created” (Prov 8.22), we must not understand that the entire Word is a creature by nature, but that he clothed himself in the created body and that God has created him for our sake — for us he prepared for him the created body (Heb 10.5), as it is written, so that in him we might be able to become renewed and deified


[p. 604] {#po.56.p604}
начѧткѫ ємѫ быти. ꙗкоⷤⷧ быти сѫпротивьнѫ, єже рєшти ꙁъдалъ єсты породѫ. а глаголємое начѧтокъ пѫтии, єже быти емѫ єдиночѧднѫ словєси. εἶναι ἐναντίον τὸ μὲν ἔκτισε τῷ γεννήματι, τὸ λεγόμενον ἀρχὴν ὅδῶν τῷ εἷναι αὐτὸν μονογενῆ Λόγον.
3. What then deceived you, mindless men, to call the creator a creature? Or where did you buy for yourselves this empty thought to show off with it? For the Proverbs say “he has created” (Prov 8.22), yet they do not say that the Son is creature, but offspring. And, according to the previously stated distinction in Scriptures regarding “he has created” and “creature”, they recognize what is by nature proper to the Son — that he is the only-begotten Wisdom and framer of the creatures; and when they say “he has created”, they say it not about his essence, but that he became a beginning of many ways, so that “he
has created” stands in contrast to the offspring, and the saying the “beginning of ways” (Prov 8.22) (stands in contrast to) his being the only-begotten Word

49. 1. “From the visible creation we see his invisible things understood from what is made” (Rom 1.20), and here too we see not every single being by itself, and not one first and another second, but all (of them) together as they were made according to their kind. For the apostle does not seek them individually, so as to say “whether angel, or throne, or dominion, or authority”, but everything together, all according to their kind, “whether angels, or archangels, or principalities” (Col 1.16), for such is the origin of the creatures.

50. 1. Your often-resounded assertion then — that the Son is a creature — is not true but is merely your fantasy. You are convicted by Solomon whom you slandered many times. 4. And this again can be reasoned from the passage itself. Being Son and having God for his Father (for he is his proper offspring), he nevertheless calls Father Lord, (and this is) not because he was his slave, but because he “assumed the image of the slave” (Phil 2.7). For it was fitting for him who was the Word from the Father to name God Father, because this is proper to son with regard to father. Thus, he “came to finish the work” (Jn 4.34) and “assumed the image of the slave” to name the Father Lord.

6. For in David’s prayer too the Holy Spirit indicates the same distinction and says (in) the Psalms, “Give your might to your child and save the Son of your maidservant’ (Ps 85.16). For the natural child of God is one thing, and the children of the maidservant, which is the nature of things originate, is (totally) another. Therefore, the Son has the Father's might as well, whereas the others require salvation.

51. 1. But if, because he was called “slave” [“son” gr.], they talk nonsense, let them know that both Isaac was called Abraham’s slave [son gr.] (Gen 21.7), and the son of the Sumanitis was called slave (4 Kings 4.18). Indeed then, we are slaves, (and) when he became as we, he too calls the Father Lord, as we do. And this he did out of love to man, so that we too, being slaves by nature and receiving “the Spirit of the Son” (Gal 4.6), might dare to call him by grace Father, who is by nature our Lord.

For things that are formed and created are made first of all in order to be and exist, and (it is) second to their being created to do whatever the Lord will command them, as such things may be seen with regard to all things.

5. Therefore here too we must understand the same thing. For you see that (the Word) is not created into essence [existence gr-], but, “in the beginning was the Word” (Jn 1.1), while afterwards he is sent “for the works” (Prov 8.22) and for the economy towards them. For even before the works came to exist, the Son was always, nor was there yet need for him to be created. 6. But when everything was created, and afterwards came a need in economy for their correction, then the Word gives himself in order to come down and become like his works, which he communicated to us in the phrase “he created”. And through the prophet Isaiah, seeking to show (us) the like, he says again, “And now thus says the Lord, who formed me from the womb to be his slave, to assemble Jacob for Israel. | will be assembled and glorified before the Lord” (Isa 49.5).

3. As an example, [+ if gr.] a certain son, when (his) subjects were lost [+ and gr.] (taken captive) by the enemy due to their own carelessness, and need was urgent, was to be sent by (his) father to recover and bring them back, and on setting out was to clothe himself in dress similar to theirs and fashion himself as they — lest the kidnappers, recognizing him as the master, should flee and prevent his arrival down to where they hid (his subjects) —, and then if anyone were to ask him 4. why he did so would (hear) him say, “My Father thus has formed and prepared me for his works”, (it must be clear) that in saying it like this, he neither implies that he is a slave or one of the works, nor speaks of the beginning of his origin, but of the economy that was given to him for the works afterwards. Ὡσπερ δὲ ἦν αὐτὸς πρὶν Ἀβραὰμ γενέσθαι, ὁ δὲ Ἰσραὴλ μετὰ τὸν Ἀβραὰμ γέγονε, καὶ δῆλόν ἐστιν, ὅτι προῧπάρχων ὕστερον πλάττεται, καὶ ἡ πλάσις οὐ τὴν ἀρχὴν τοῦ εἶναι, ἀλλὰ τὴν ἐνανθρώπησιν σημαίνει, ἐν ᾖ καὶ ἐπισυνάγει τὰς φυλὰς τοῦ Ἰσραήλ· οὕτως ἁ̓́ρα ἀεὶ συνὼν τῷ Πατρὶ, αὐτὸς τῆς κτίσεως δημιουργός ἐστι, καὶ δῆλόν ἐστιν ὅτι δεύτερό ἐστιν αὐτοῦ τὰ ἔργα, καὶ τὸ, ἔκτισεν, οὐκ ἀρχὴν τοῦ εῖναι αὐτὸν, ἀλλὸ τὴν εἰς τὰ ἔργα γενομένην οἰκονομίαν, ἧν ἐν τῇ σαρκὶ πεποίηκε γωρίζει
2. For as he himself had been before Abraham was born, while Israel came into being after Abraham, and it is clear that he exists first and is formed afterwards, and (his) forming points not to (his) beginning of existence but to (his) humanity (taken later), by which he also assembles the tribes of Israel, so then being that way always with the Father, he himself is framer of the creation. His works are later than he himself, and “he has created” indicates not the beginning of his existence, but the economy that took place with respect to the works which he did in flesh.
Подобааше бо не того сѫшта є҃ дѣлѣⷯ, нъ пачеl съдѣтелѧ его сѫшта ихъ. и ѻбновение тѣхъ на сѧ приѧти. да томѫ ꙁиждемѫ въ насъ. все въ сєбѣ въꙁиждеть. и глаголѧ бо ꙁъдалъ, абїе и винѫ прирєчє глаголѧ. дѣла, да еже ꙁъдатиісѧ въ дѣлѣхъ. Єже быти чловѣкѫ оукажеιть на обновєниє ихъ, Ἐπρεπ-εγὰρ, ἅλλον αὐτὸν ὅντα τῶν ἔργων, καὶ μᾶλλον δημιουργὸν αὐτῶν ὅντα αὐτὸν, καὶ τὴν τούτων ἀνανέωσιν εἰς ἑαυτὸν ἀναδέξασθαι. ἵνα, αὐτοῦ κτιζομένου εἱ̓ς ἡμᾶς, τὰ πάντα εἰς ἑαυτὸν ἀνακτίσηται. Καὶ γὰρ λέγων, ἔκτισεν, εὐθὺς καὶ τὴν αἰτίαν ἐπήγαγε, λέγων, τὰ ἔργα, ἵνα τὸ, εἰς τὰ ἔργα κτίζεσθαι, τὸ γίνεσθαι ἄνθρωπον δηλώσῃ εἰς τὴν τούτων ἀνανέωσιν.
3. For it was fitting (for him), being other than his works, but rather their framer, to take on himself their renewal, so that, being created for us, he could re-create all things in himself. For when he said, “he created”, (he) immediately added the reason by saying, “the works”, so that his being created for the works might point to his becoming man for their renewal.

5. For it is written, “In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God” (Jn 1.1), and it does not say what for. But when “the Word became flesh” (Jn 1.14), then it sets down the reason why this became so, saying, “and dwelt among us” (Jn 1.14). And again in saying, “he who was in the image of God”, the apostle has not set down the reason, but when “he took on him the form of the slave”, then he adds, saying, “he has humbled himself unto death, even the death of the cross” (Phil 2.6.7.8), for it was for this that he both became flesh and took the image of a slave.

Παῦλος γοῦν ἀφωρισμένος ἀπόστολος εἰς Εὐαγγέλιον, ὃ προεπηγγείλατο ὁ Κύριος διὰ τῶν προφητῶν, εἶχε πρὸ ἑαυτοῦ τὸ Εὐαγγέλιον, οὗ καὶ γέγονε διάκονος· καὶ ὁ Ἰωάννης μὲν, προχειρισθεὶς Κύριος οὐκ ἔχων πρὸ έαυτοῦ αἰτίαν τοῦ εἶναι Λόγος, ἣ μόνον ὅτι τοῦ Πατρός ἐστι γέννημα καὶ σοφία μονογενὴς, ὅταν ἄνθρωπος γίνεται τότε καὶ τὴν αἰτίαν τίθησι, δι᾽ ἧν μέλλει σάρκα φορεῖν. Προηγεῖτα γὰρ τοῦ γενέσθαι αὐτὸν ἄνθρωπον ἡ τῶν ἀνθρώπων χρεία, ἧς ἅνευ οὐκ ἂν ἐνεδύσατο σάρκα
2. Paul then, being “an apostle set apart for the Gospel, which the Lord had promised by the Prophets” (Rom 1.1-2), had the Gospel as existing prior to him, “of which he came to be minister” (Eph 3.7). And John, being set to prepare the way of the Lord, had the Lord as existing prior to him (Jn 1.30). But the Lord, not having any reason for why he must be Word, except that he is the Father's offspring and only-begotten Wisdom, when he becomes man, then sets the reason for which he must bear the flesh. For the human need preceded his becoming man, without which he would not have clothed himself in flesh.

56. 1. For if, according to them, since the essence of the Word is created, he, being a creature, says, “The Lord created me’, (then) he was not created for us. And since he was not created for us, we are not created in him. And if not created in him, we do not have him within ourselves but have (him) externally, as if perhaps (receiving) a teaching from him as from a teacher. That being the case with us, sin in no lesser way than before continues to reign (Rom 5.21) over the flesh, indwelling (it) and not being expelled from it.

57. 1. Thus, regarding creation Moses did not say, “In the beginning he begot’, nor “In the beginning was’, but “In the beginning God created the heaven and the earth” (Gen 1.1). Nor did David sing, “Your hands begot me’, but “made me” (Ps 118.73), everywhere saying the phrase “he has made me’ with respect to the creatures, 2. while (describing) the Son differently. #p# For he did not say “I made”, but “I begot’ (Ps 109.3), and “he begets me” (Prov 8.25), and “my heart uttered a good Word” (Ps 44.2).
sets down the reason — the works. But when he indicates simply the generation that is from the Father, he immediately adds by saying, “Before all the hills, he begets me” (Prov 8.25). the “Word was God” (Jn 1.1). And his becoming man would not have taken place, had not the human need become the reason. 6. The Son then is not a creature. For if he were a creature, he would not have said “he begets me”. The creatures then are from without, they are works of the maker, while the offspring is not from without as a work, but from the Father (and) proper to (his) essence; those are creatures, but this one is the Word of God, the only-begotten Son.

3. And this difference is that the creatures are made upon the beginning and have a distanced beginning of existence. Therefore also what is said of them, “In the beginning he made” (Gen 1.1), is again equal to saying of them, “From the beginning he has made’. The Lord, knowing that which he had made, pointed out when he convicted the pharisees by saying, “He who made them from the beginning, made them male and female” (Mt 19.4), for from one beginning, when they were not yet, did originate things came to be and were created.

This too the Spirit signified in the Psalms, saying, “And you, Lord, at the beginning founded the earth” (Ps 101.26), and again he said, “Remember your assembly that you have obtained from the beginning” (Ps 73.2). Now, it is evident that what occurs at the beginning has a beginning of creation, and that [+ from some gr.] beginning God obtained his congregation. And that the phrase “In the beginning he made” (Gen 1.1) — with its word “made” —, means “began to make”, Moses himself shows after the completion of everything by saying, “And God has blessed the seventh day and has sanctified (it), because in it he rested from all his works which God began to make” (Gen 2.3).

5. So then the creatures began to be made, whereas God’s Word, not having beginning of existence, did not really have beginning of existence, nor began to come to be, but was always. But the works have beginning in (their) making, and their beginning is prior to their coming to be. The Word, not being of things that come to be, rather comes to be himself the framer of things that have a beginning.

58. 1. So then divine Scriptures recognize the difference between the offspring and things made. They teach that the offspring is Son, not begun from any beginning but eternal, but as for the things made, they show them to be an external work of the maker as those that had a beginning whenever they came to be. In this way, John theologizing about the Son and recognizing the difference between the phrases, did not say, “In the beginning he became” and “he was made’, but “In the beginning was the Word” (Jn 1.1), so that we might hear “offspring”

3. (Your) first passage is this, “Is not he your Father who created you? Did he not make you and create you” (Deut 32.6)? And slightly thereafter in the same Ode he says, “God who begot you, you abandoned, and forsook God who nourished you” (Deut 32.18). The meaning (here) is remarkable, for he does not say first “He begot” lest the phrase be taken interchangeably with “he made”, and they might find a pretense for saying, “Well, Moses first said it this way — that ‘God said, let us make man’ (Gen 1.26) — but then again he said himself, ‘God who begot you, you abandoned’ (Deut 32.18), as if the phrases were interchangeable, for offspring and work are identical. [+ But gr.] after the words ‘created’ and ‘made’ he added lastly ‘begot’, so that the phrase might reveal (its own) explanation.”

And generally, when the Scriptures want to point to a son, (they do so) not by the word “created”, but by the word “begot”.

God the creator becomes our Father as well. Therefore, it is the name “Father”, not “creature”, that is proper to the Son, whereas the name “Son” is proper to the Father.

Διὰ τοῦτο γοῦν ἡμεῖς οὐ πρῶτον γεννώμεθα, ἀλλὰ ποιούμεθα γέγραπται γάρ· Ποιήσωμεν ἄνθρωπον· ὕστερον δὲ, δεξάμενοι τὴν τοῦ Πνεύματος χάριν, λεγόμεθα τότε λοιπὸν καὶ γεννᾶσθαι. Ἀμέλει καὶ ἐν τῆ Ὡδῆ Μωσῆς ὁ μέγας μετὰ διανοίας καλῆς πρῶτον τὸ, ἐκτήσατο, καὶ ὕστερον τὸ, ἐγέννησεν, εἴρηκεν, ὑπὲρ τοῦ μὴ, ἀκούσαντας τὸ, ἐγέννησεν, ἐπιλαθέσθαι αὐτοὺς τῆς ἐξ ἀρχῆς ἑαυτῶν φύσεως, ἀλλ᾽ίνα γινώσκωσιν, ὅτι ἐξ ἀρχῆς μέν εἰσι κτίσματα, ὅταν δὲ κατὰ χάριν λέγωνται γεννᾶσθαι ὡς υἱοὶ, ἀλλ᾽ οὐδὲν ἦττόν εἰσι πάλιν οἱ ἅνθρωποι ποιήματα κατὸ φύσιν.
6. And may this show that it is not we who are sons by nature but the Son, who is in us. And again God is not our natural Father but of the Word, who is in us, in whom and by whom “we cry, Abba, Father” (Rom 8.15). And so in like manner, the Father, in whomever he sees his own Son, those he himself calls sons and says, “I begot”, since begetting signifies a Son, and making reveals the deeds.
7. For that reason, we too are not begotten first but made, for it is written, “Let us make man” (Gen 1.26), while afterwards, on receiving the spiritual grace, we are said then to be begotten also. Thus, in his Ode too great Moses with a good meaning says first, “he has created” (Deut 32.6), and afterwards, “he has begotten” (Deut 32.18), lest, hearing “he begot’, they might forget their original nature, but that they might know that from the beginning they are creatures, yet when by grace they are said to be begotten as sons, they are again no less than before, men, works by nature.


[p. 652] {#po.56.p652}
60. А ꙗко нє тождє єсть ꙁъданїке єжє и пороⷧ҇. нъ растоістоита отъ сєбє. родомъ же и наꙁнамєнанїⷩ бєсѣдъ. самъ г҃ь въ самѣхъ притчаⷯ҇ оукаꙁаеть. рєк бо г҃ь съꙁдалъ мѧ начѧтъкъ пѫти свойⷯ, прирєчє. прѣждеⷩⷤ всѣⷯ хлъмъ раждаєть 60. Ὁτι δὲ οὐ ταὐτόν ἐστι κτίσμα καὶ γέννημα, ἀλλὰ διεστήκασιν ἀλλήλων τῇ τε φύσει καὶ τῆ ἐκ τῶν λέξεων σημασίᾳ, αὐτὸς ὁ Κύριος ἐν αὐταῖς ταῖς Παροιμίαις δείκνυσιν. О словєсиⷩ пакы инако отьць сы єго роідѡ б҃ъ, бываєть потоⷨ, и ꙁиждитєль єго и твоірьць. Єгда въ ꙁъданоѭ, и сътворєноѭ Облѣчєⷩ҇ісѧ плъть слово и бѫдєть чловѣкъ. 61. Τοιαύτης τοίνυν οὗσης τῆς διαφορᾶς τοῦ, ἔκτισε, καὶ τοῦ, γεννᾶ με, καὶ, τῆς ἀρχῆς ὁδῶν, καὶ τοῦ, πρὸ πάντων· τῶν μὲν ἀνθρώπων κτίστης ὣν ὁ θεὸς, οὕτως, ὥσπερ εἴρηται, γίνεται καὶ ὕστερον Πατὴρ διὰ τὸν ἐν αὐτοῖς οἰκοῦντα Λόγον αὐτοῦ. Ἐπὶ δὲ τοῦ Λόγου τὸ ἔμπαλιν Πατὴρ γὰρ ὠ̔̀ν αὐτοῦ φύσει ὁ Θεὸς, γίνεται μετὰ ταῦτα καὶ κτίστης αὐτοῦ καὶ ποιητὴς, ὅταν τὴν κτισθεῖσαν καὶ ποιηθεῖσαν ἐνδύσηται σάρκα ὁ Λόγος, καὶ γένηται ἄνθρωπος.
61. 1. Such then being the difference between “he created” and “he begot me”, and between “beginning of ways” and “before all”, God, being creator, afterwards, as was said, becomes Father (of men) because of his Word that lives in them. But regarding the Word, again, this is different. God, being his Father by nature, afterwards becomes both his creator and maker, when the Word clothes himself in flesh that is created and made, and becomes man.

For in it (i.e. Christ's body), the Lord becomes our guide to the kingdom of heaven and to his Father, saying “I am the way” (Jn 14.6) and “the door” (Jn 10.9), and “through me” all must “enter” (Jn 10.1). Therefore, he
is also said to be “first-born from the dead” (Col 1.18), not because he died before us, for we had died first, but because having taken death for us and “abolished” (2 Tim 1.10) it, he was the first to rise as man, for our sakes raising his body. Now that he has already risen, we too (2 Cor 4.14) because of him rise from the dead.

Єдиночѧдъи бо, не сѫштемъ инѣмъ братроⷧ єдночѧⷣ єсть. прьвѣньць же ꙁа ины братриѧ глаіголєтьсѧ прьвѣнєць 62. Εὶ δὲ καὶ πρωτότοκος τῆς κτίσεως λέγεται, ἀλλʼ οὐχ ὡς ἐξισούμενος τοῖς κτίσμασι, καὶ πρῶτος αὐτῶν κατὰ χρόνον πρωτότοκος λέγεται· (πῶς γὰρ, ὅπου γε Μονογενής ἐστιν αὐτός; ἀλλὰ διὰ τὴν πρὸς τὰ κτίσματα συγκατάβασιν τοῦ Λόγου, καθ᾽ ἣν καὶ πολλῶν γέγονεν ἀδελφός. Ὁ γάρ τοι μονογενὴς, οὐκ ὅντων ἅλλων ἀδελφῶν, μονογενής ἐστιν· ὁ δὲ πρωτότοκος διὰ τοὺς ἁ̓́λλους ἀδελφοὺς πρωτότοκος λέγεται
62. 1. But if he is also called “first-born of works” (Col 1.15), still this is not (to say) that he was made equal with the creatures and is said to be first-born only in terms of time — for how can this be (if) he is only-begotten? —, but it is because of the humiliation of the Word towards creatures by which he became “the brother of many”. For only-begotten is (called) only-begotten when there are no other brothers, but first-born is said to be first-born because of other brothers.

2. For this reason then, it is nowhere written in the Scriptures “the first-born of God” nor “the creature of God”, but “only-begotten”, and “Son”, and “Word”, and “Wisdom” — all in reference to him being proper to the Father. For “we saw his glory, the glory as of the only-begotten of the Father” (Jn 1.14); and “God sent his only-begotten Son” (1 Jn 4.9); and “Lord, your Word remains forever” (Ps 118.89): and “In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God” (Jn 1.1); and “Christ God’s power and God's Wisdom” (1 Cor 1.24); and “This is my beloved Son” (Mt 3.17); and “You are Christ, the Son of the living God” (Mt 16.16).

Οὐ δύναται γὰρ ὁ αὐτὸς Μονογενής τε καὶ πρωτότοκος εῖναι, εἰ μὴ ἅρα πρὸς ἅλλο καὶ ἅλλο· ἵνα Μονογενὴς μὲν διὰ τὴν ἐκ Πατρὸς γέννησιν, ὥσπερ εἵρηται, πρωτότοκος δὲ διὰ τὴν εἳς τὴν κτίσιν συγκατάβασιν, καὶ τὴν τῶν πολλῶν ἀδελφοποίησιν
3. But the term “first-born” (Col 1.15) has to do with the humiliation towards the work, for because of it he was called first-born; and the phrase “he created” (Prov 8.22) has to do with the grace for the works, for he is created for them. If he is only-begotten, then let the term “first-born” be explained, but if he is first-born, then let him not be only-begotten. For one and the same cannot be both only-begotten and first-born, except when they are related as being different to one another, so that only-begotten is (such) in virtue of (his) generation that is from the Father, as was said, while first-born is (such) in virtue of his descent to the work.

other, everyone should say then that the characteristic of only-begotten correctly holds the preference with respect to the Word because there is no other Word or other Wisdom, but he alone is the genuine Son of the Father. For, as was previously said, not in connection with a certain reason, but simply (i.e. with no qualifier) it is said of him, “The only-begotten Son who is in the bosom of the Father” (Jn 1.18). But “first-born” again has a creaturely reason that is connected with it, which Paul [supplied gr.] in saying that “in him all things were created” (Col 1.16). If all the creatures were created in him, he is other than the creatures and is not a creature, but the creator of all.

Given that such thinking is unseemly, they are convicted by the truth in that he is called “first-born among many brothers” (Rom 8.29) because of the kinship to the flesh, whereas (he is called) “first-born from the dead” (Col 1.18) because from him and after him is the rising of the dead, and (he is called) “first-born of the entire work” (Col 1.15) because of the Father's love for humanity, which (made it possible that) through his Word not only “all things are constituted”, but the work itself, of which the apostle said, “waiting for the revelation of the children of God, will become free” at some point “from the bondage of corruption into freedom of glory of the children of God” (Rom 8.19.21).

тѣлєси бо свое бѣ смр҃ть. и ꙗкоже тѣлєси, | * своє єсть съмрьть · такождє тѣлєснааго єстыства. своє оубо бѫдеть, глаголємоє съꙁъдалꙑ мѧ начатъкъ пѫтии своиⷯ. съпасоу бо сице по плъти ꙁъданѫ бывъшѫ. и начѧтъкѫ бываѭштѫ въꙁиждємый. и имѫштѫ нашь начѧтъкъ. ижє приꙗ чловѣчьскѫѭ плъть, по лѣпотѣ и лѭдїе нѣции по нємъ ꙁиждѫтьсѧ. глаголѭштоу д҃в҃доу· напсанъ съ бѫди въ родъ инъ, и лѭѫдиє ꙁиждємии въсхвалѧть г҃а҃, и пакы, въꙁвѣштєно бѫдєть господєви родъ грѧдыи. и въꙁвѣстѧть правдѫ єго, лѭдємъ родивъшиимъсѧ Τοῦ γὰρ σώματος ἵδιον ἦν ὁ θάνατος· καὶ ὥσπερ τοῦ σώματος ἵδιός ἐστιν ὁ θάνατος, οὕτω τῆς σωματικῆς παρουσίας ἴδιον ἃν εἰη τὸ λεγόμενον· Κύριος ἔκτισέ με ἀρχὴν όδῶν αύτοῦ. 2. For to the body death was proper, and just as to the body death is proper, in the same way to the bodily essence [coming gr.] it will be proper to say, “The Lord created me a beginning of his ways” (Prov 8.22). For when the Savior was created this way according to the flesh, and became a beginning of things re-created, and had our beginning — hat human flesh which he assumed ---, it is fitting that after him also some people are created, as David says, “Let this be written for another generation, and the people that will be created will praise the Lord” (Ps 101.19), and again, “The coming generation will be reported to the Lord, and they will proclaim his righteousness to a people that will be born whom the Lord has made” (Ps 21.30-31).
“He is the head of the ecclesiastical body, who is the beginning, the first-born from the dead, so that in all things he himself might be preeminent” (Col 1.18).

3. For we will not hear any more, “In the day that you eat from it, you will surely die” (Gen 2.17), but “Where | am, there you will be also” (Jn 14.3), so that we may say, “We are his work created for kind deeds” (Eph 2.10). And again, since the work of God, namely, man, after being created perfect has become deficient through the transgression and dead by sin (Eph 2.1), and it was unseemly that the work of God should remain imperfect — therefore, all the saints were praying about this, (as for example) in Psalm 137, saying, “The Lord will requite for me. Lord, do not despise the works of your hand” (Ps 137.8) —, 4. for that reason the perfect Word of God clothes himself in an imperfect body, and is said to be created “for the works”, so that (after) giving back the debt for us, he might, by himself, perfect what was deficient in man.

68. 1. But they might say: “even though the Savior were a creature, God could simply speak (the word) and invalidate the curse’. For he could have destroyed, even before the ark of Noah, those who transgressed at that time, but he has done it after the ark. He could have also, even before Moses appeared, just spoken (the word) and have led the people out of Egypt, but it was profitable (to do it) through Moses. And God could have saved (his) people without the judges but it was profitable that for a certain time judges should be raised up for them. 3. For what he does, that (action) profits men, and no other way would have been fitting; and what is profitable and fitting, of that he takes care. Therefore, he could have spoken out the Law from heaven, (but) he saw that it was more profitable to men if he speaks from Sinai. And he has done such a thing so that Moses could make the ascent, (allowing) others to hear the word close to them, so that they would surely believe.

70. 1. But this would not have taken place, if the Word had been a creature. For with a creature, the devil, himself being a creature, would have always waged the war, and man, being in between the two, would have been always sentenced to death, having no one through whom and in whom he might be united to God (and) be free from every sin [fear gr.]. Thus, the truth reveals that the Word is not of things originate, but rather is the framer of all those things. For this way, he took on the originate and human body, so that having renewed it as (its) framer, he might deify it in himself, and this way might bring us all into the heavenly kingdom after his likeness. For man would not have been deified if united to a creature (and) if the Son were not the genuine God. Nor would man have been presented to the Father, if he had not been his natural and genuine Word who had clothed himself in the body. 2. And as we would not have been freed from sin and from the curse, if it had not been by nature human flesh in which the Word clothed himself — for there would have been nothing in common for us with what is foreign —, so man would not have been deified, if the Word who became flesh (Jn 1.14) had not
