Πρὸς γὰρ κτίσμα, κτίσμα ὣν καὶ ὁ διάβολος, εῖχεν ἀεὶ τὴν μάχην· καὶ μέσος ὠ̔̀ν ὁ ἅνθρωπος, ὕποπτος ἦν ἀεὶ τῷ θανάτῳ, μὴ ἕχων ἐν ᾦ καὶ διʼ οὗ συναφθεὶς τῷ Θεῷ ἐλεύθερος παντὸς φόβου γένηται. Ὁθεν ἡ ἀλήθειο δείκνυσι μὴ εῖναι τῶν γενητῶν τὸν Λόγον, ἀλλὰ μᾶλλον τούτων αὐτὸν δημιουργόν· οὕτω γὰρ καὶ προσελάβετο τὸ γενητὸν καὶ ἀνθρώπινον σῶμα, ίνα, τοῦτο ὡς δημιουργὸς ἀνακαινίσας, ἐν ἑαυτῷ θεοποιήση καὶ οὕτως εἰς βασιλείαν οὐρανῶν εἰσαγάγῃ πάντας ἡμᾶς καθ ὁμοιότητα ἐκείνου. Οὺκ ὰν δὲ πάλιν ἐθεοποιήθη κτίσματι συναφθεὶς ὁ ἄνθρωπος, εἰ μὴ Θεὸς ἦν ἀληθινὸς ὁ Υἱός· καὶ οὐκ ἂν παρέστη τῷ Πατρὶ ὁ ἅνθρωπος, εὶ μὴ φύσει καὶ ἀληθινὸς ἧν αὐτοῦ Λόγος ὁ ἐνδυσάμενος τὸ σῶμα. Καὶ ὥσπερ οὐκ ἂν ἡ̓λευθερώθημεν ἀπὸ τῆς ἀμαρτίας καὶ τῆς κατάρας, εἰ μὴ φύσει σὰρξ ἦν ἀνθρωπίνη, ἧν ἐνεδύσατο ὁ Λόγος· οὐδὲν γὰρ κοινὸν ἡ̓͂ν ἡμῖν πρὸς τὸ ἀλλότριον οὕτως οὐκ ἂν ἐθεοποιήθη ὁ ἄνθρωπος, εἰ μὴ φύσει ἐκ τοῦ Πατρὸς καὶ
been by nature from the Father and genuine and proper to him. For this kind of union happened, so that he who is in the nature of the divinity might unite (to himself) what is man by nature, and his salvation and deification might be firm.

3. Therefore, let those who reject that the Son is by nature from the Father and is proper to (his) essence, reject also the fact that he assumed the genuine human flesh from Mary Ever-virgin. For there would have equally been no profit to men, whether the Word of God were not natural and genuine Son, or the flesh were not genuine, which he has assumed. But he did take genuine flesh, though Valentinus rages, for the Word was by nature genuine God, though Ario-maniacs rage. But the Word is not work, nor of those that praise, but is praised with the Father, and he accepts worship and is theologized, being his Word and Wisdom, and of the works the framer. This too the Spirit spoke in the Psalms with a good distinction that “the Word of the Lord is straight, and all his works are faithful” (Ps 32.4), as in another one too he says, “How magnificent are your works, Lord! Through Wisdom you made everything” (Ps 103.24).

2. Of this distinction Paul too speaks, writing to the Hebrews, “The Word of God is living and active, and sharper than any two-edged sword, and reaching to the dividing of soul and spirit, even joints and marrow, and a discerner of the thoughts and intents of the heart, and neither is there any creature invisible before him, but all things are naked and open before the eyes of him to whom is our account” (Heb 4.12-13). For behold, he calls things originate “creature”, but the Son he recognizes as the Word of God, as if he is other than the creatures. And again, in saying, “all things are naked and open before the eyes of him to whom is our account”, he shows him to be other than all (of them).

But if the entire work groans together for the freedom from the bondage of corruption, while the Son is not of those who groan nor of those who require freedom, but himself is the one who brings about sonship and delivers all, saying to the Jews, “But the slave does not remain in the house for ages, [+ yet the Son remains for ages gr]; if the the Son makes you free, you will truly be free” (Jn 8.35-36), then from this point it is clearer than the light that the Word of God is not a creature but the genuine and by nature proper Son of the Father.

As for the statement, “The Lord created me a beginning of his ways” (Prov 8.22), even though concisely, but, as | think, enough has been suggested to cause the learned to construct [+ stronger gr.] refutations of the Arian heresy. But since the Arians, reading (the verse) that follows afterwards, view this in an evil way also, because it is written, “He has founded me before the ages” (Prov 8.23), as if this is said of the divinity of the Word and not of his coming as a human, itis necessary, in interpreting this verse also, to show their deceit.

73. 1. It is written, “The Lord by Wisdom has founded the earth” (Prov 3.19). If then by Wisdom he has founded the earth, how can he who founds be founded? But this too is said proverbially, and therefore we [+ must gr.] investigate its meaning as well, so that we might know that, while through Wisdom the Father frames and founds the earth to be firm and steadfast, Wisdom itself is founded for us, so that it might become beginning and foundation of our new creation and renewal.

For it was regarding this point that when the Apostles were asked, Peter responded, saying, “You are Christ, the Son of the living God” (Mt 16.16).

74. 1. The Arians then, in imitation of their own father the devil, must inquire whether he said, “He founded me to be Word or Son’, (if they want) to hold what they hold. But if he did not say such things, let them not invent for themselves what is not. For he did not say, “Before the world he has founded me as Word or Son”, but simply, “He founded me’ (Prov 8.23), to show again, as | said, that it is not for his own sake but for those who are founded on him, that he says this in the manner of proverbs.

4. But just as the heretics think in the human manner, they must be refuted with human examples from the same words (they use). He did not say, “He has made me a foundation”, lest he might (seem) to be made and to have a beginning of being, and they might in this way find a shameless occasion for impiety, but “He has founded me”.

5. And what is founded is founded for the sake of the stones that are placed on it. And it happens not simply, but when a stone is brought from the mountain and set down in the depth of the earth. But when the need comes, and it is brought (down) and enters into the depth of the earth, then [+ if gr.] the stone could have a voice, it would then say, “Now he has founded me by bringing (me) from the mountain”.

75. 1. And as for the phrases “before the ages” (Prov 8.23) and “before he made the earth” (Prov 8.24), and “before the mountains were established” (Prov 8.25), let them not confuse any one. For he (ie. the Word) has linked these very well with “he founded” and “he created”, because this again refers to the economy according to the flesh.
6. Therefore, the Lord also, when founded, did not have a beginning of existence, for he was the Word before that, but when he clothed himself in our body, which he severed and took from Mary, then he said, “He has founded me”, which is exactly as to say, “Me, being the Word, he has clothed in the earthly body.” For in this way he is founded for our sake, taking on him all that is ours, so that we, as incorporated, and conformed, and bound together in him through the likeness of the flesh, may reach to “a perfect man” (Eph 4.13), and abide immortal [+ and gr_] incorruptible.

The God of all then, creating us by his own Word, and knowing what belongs to us better than we, and foreseeing that, being made “good” (Gen 1.31), we would afterwards become the transgressors of the commandment and be cast out of paradise for disobedience, he who loves man and is kind, prepared beforehand through his own Word, by whom he had also created us, the economy of our salvation, so that though by the serpent’s deceit we fell (from him), we might not remain dead to the very end, but having in the Word the deliverance and salvation prepared for us beforehand, we might rise again and abide immortal, when he is created “a beginning of the ways” (Prov 8.22), and being “first-born of work” (Col 1.15) becomes the “first-born of the brothers” (Rom 8.29), and himself rises (as) “the beginning of the dead” (1 Cor 15.20).

6. For who, on hearing the framing Wisdom say, “The Lord created me for his works” (Prov 8.22), does not immediately search for the meaning, pondering how can that which is created itself create? Who, on hearing the only-begotten Son of God say that he was created “a beginning of ways” (Prov 8.22), does not examine the meaning, wondering how the only-begotten can become a beginning of many others? For it is a proverb, but “a thoughtful man” (Prov 1.5), he said, “will understand the proverb and a dark word, the sayings of the wise and riddles” (Prov. 1.6).

3. Such an image of Wisdom being then created in us and being in all the works, in truth did the genuine and framing Wisdom assume to itself what related to its own image and said, “The Lord created me for his works” (Prov 8.22). For the things that the wisdom spoke in us, those same things the Lord himself speaks as if they were his own, and, while he is not himself created, being creator, yet because of the image created of him in the works, he says those things on account of them, as if of himself. And as the Lord himself said, “Anyone who receives you, receives me” (Mt 10.40), because his image is in us, though he is not among the creatures, yet because his image and imprint is created in the works, so he says, as if himself being (that image and imprint), “The Lord created me a beginning of his ways for his works” (Prov 8.22).

that, as | said, the world might recognize [+ in it gr_] its own framer the Word, and through him the Father. And this is what Paul says, “Because that which is known of God should be manifest in them, for God has shown it to them. For the invisible things of him from the creation of the world are understood by his work as being visible” (Rom 1.19-20). Therefore, the Word is not a creature in essence, but it is of that which is in us and spoken as Wisdom that the passage talks in Proverbs (Prov 8.22).

79. 1. But if to this they do not believe either, let them tell us themselves, whether there is any wisdom in the creatures or not? And if not, how is it that the apostle makes a reproach, saying, “Since in the Wisdom of God the world did not recognize God by wisdom” (1 Cor 1.21)? Or how is it, if there is no wisdom that a “multitude of wise men” (Wis 6.24) are found in Scriptures? And “a wise man fears and avoids evil” (Prov 14.16); and “with wisdom is a house built” (Prov 24.3); and Ecclesiastes says, “Human wisdom will enlighten his face” (Eccl 8.1); and he reprimands the impertinent men, saying, “Do not say, what made it happen that the former days were better than these? For you did not inquire about this in wisdom” (Eccl 7.10).

If men bear it within them, they will recognize the true Wisdom of God and will know that they are made truly “according to the image of God” (Gen 1.26). my name is re-created in the works”. But in saying this, he is not teaching that his own essence is re-created. Rather, (what is re-created) is his own image by means of (his) name. But again it should not be surprising that the Son speaks of his image in us as if it were himself, considering that — we will not grow weary to say the same thing — when Saul was at one time persecuting the Church, in which was his imprint and image, he said, as if he were himself persecuted, “Saul, why do you persecute me” (Acts 9.4)?

2. Therefore, as was said, if it were the image of Wisdom itself, as being present in the works, that had stated, “The Lord created me for the works” (Prov 8.22), no one would have been surprised. Similarly, if the true and framing Wisdom itself — the only-begotten Word of God — speaks of its own image [+ as if about itself gr.], “The Lord created me for the works’ (Prov. 8.22), let no one bypass the wisdom re-created in the world and in the works, so as to think that “he created me” is said of the essence of the very Wisdom, lest “mixing the wine with water” (Isa 1.22), he robs the truth.

3. For it (i.e. Wisdom) is creative and framing, but its image is re-created in the works as the image of the image. And he says “beginning of ways”, because such wisdom becomes a sort of beginning and antecedent inscription of the knowledge of God. For someone then stepping, as it were, on this path first, and keeping it in the fear of God — as Solomon said, “The fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom” (Prov 1.7) —, [+ then gr.] ascending in his mind and discerning the framing Wisdom in the work, will discern in it also its Father, as the Lord himself said, “Anyone who saw me, has seen the Father” (Jn 14.9), and as John writes, “Anyone who confesses the Son, has the Father also” (1 Jn 2.23).

4. He said, “Before the ages he has founded me” (Prov 8.23). because in such image the works remain firm and eternal. [+ Then gr.], lest anyone, hearing of the wisdom created that way in the works, should imagine the true Wisdom, God's Son, to be some kind of creature, it was necessary (for him) to add, “before the mountains” (Prov 8.25); and “before the earth” (Prov 8.24); and “before the waters” (Prov 8.24); and “before all the hills, he begets me” (Prov 8.25), so that in saying, “before the entire work” — for he has indicated the entire work by these statements —, he might show that he is not created together with the works according to essence.

Εἷτα ἵνα μή τις, ἀκούων περὶ τῆς οὕτως ἐν τοῖς ἔργοις κτισθείσης σοφίας, νομίσῃ τὴν ἀληθινὴν Σοφίαν τὸν τοῦ Θεοῦ Yἱὸν εῖναι τῇ φύσει κτίσμα, ἀναγκαίως ἐπήγαγε· Πρὸ τοῦ ὅρη, καὶ, πρὸ τοῦ τὴν γῆν, καὶ, πρὸ τῶν ὑδάτων, καὶ, πρὸ πάντων βουνῶν γεννᾷ με· ἵνα ἐν μὲν τῷ λέγειν πρὸ πάσης κτίσεως (τὴν γὰρ πᾶσαν κτίσιν ἐν τούτοις ἐδήλωσε·) δείξη, ὅτι μὴ κατʼ οὐσίαν συνεκτίσθη τοῖς ἔργοις.
5. For if he were created “for the works”, yet being before works, it would have been obvious that he had to be prior to the creatures. He is not then a creature by nature and essence, but — as he himself added — offspring. And as to what makes a creature different from an offspring, or how it is distinct by nature, has been shown before.

Οὐκ ἄρα τῇ φύσει καὶ οὐσίᾳ κτίσμα ἐστὶν ἀλλ᾽, ὡς αὐτὸς ἐπήγαγε, γέννημά ἐστι. Τί δὲ διαφέρει ἣ πῶς διέστηκε τὴν φύσιν κτίσμα γεννήματος, δέδεικται ἐν τοῖς ἔμπροσθεν.

Ὁ Θεὸς γὰρ αὐτοῖς ἐφανέρωσε· τὰ γὰρ ἀόρατα αὐτοῦ ἀπὸ κτίσεως κόσμου, τοῖς ποιήμασι νοούμενα καθορᾶται, ἥ τε ἀἵδιος αὐτοῦ δύναμις καὶ θεότης, εἰς τὸ εἶναι αὐτοὺς ἀναπολογήτους, διότι, γνόντες τὸν Θεὸν, οὐχ ὠς Θεὸν ἐδόξασαν, ἀλλ’ ἐλάτρευσαν τῆ κτίσει παρὰ τὸν κτίσαντα τὰ πάντα, ὅς ἐστιν εὐλογητὸς εἰς τοὺς αἴῶνας, ἀμήν. Καὶ ἐντραπήσονταί γε ἀκούοντες· Ἐπειδὴ γὰρ ἐν τῆ σοφίᾳ τοῦ Θεοῦ, κατὰ τὸν προειρημένον τρόπον, οὐκ ἔγνω ὁ̓ κόσμος διὰ τῆς σοφίας τὸν Θεὸν, ηύδόκησεν ὁ̓ Θεὸς διὰ τῆς μωρίας τοῦ κηρύγματος σῶσαι τοὺς πιστεύοντας. Οὐκέτι γὰρ, ὡς ἐν τοῖς προτέροις χρόνοις, δι εἰκόνος καὶ σκιᾶς τῆς σοφίας, τῆς ἐν τοῖς κτίσμασιν οὕσης, ἠθέλησε γινώσκεσθαι ὁ Θεός· ἀλλ’ αὐτὴν τὴν ἀληθινὴν Σοφίαν ἐποίησε σάρκα λαβεῖν, καὶ γενέσθαι ἅνθρωπον, θάνατόν τε ὑπομεῖναι σταυροῦ, ἵνα διὰ τῆς ἐν τούτῳ πίστεως πάντες λοιπὸν οἱ πιστεύοντες σώζεσθαν δύνωνται.

(Rom 1.19-21), but “served the works rather than the one who created everything, who is blessed [+ forever gr.], Amen” (Rom 1.25). 3. And they will be ashamed to hear, “Because in the wisdom of God” — in the manner we had already discussed before —, “the world did not know God by wisdom, it was pleasing to God by the foolishness of the preaching to save those who believe” (1 Cor 1.21). For no longer, as in the former times, God wanted to be known by an image and shadow (Heb 8.5) of wisdom which is in the creatures, but he has made the true Wisdom itself to take flesh and become man, and to undergo the death of the crucifixion, so that by the faith in him all who believe might now be saved (Eph 2.8).

82. 1. For the entire earth is full of the knowledge of him, because the knowledge of the Father by means of the Son and of the Son from the Father is one (Jn 14.7), and the Father delights in him (Ps 20.7), and in the same delight the Son rejoices in the Father, saying, “I was by him delighting daily, rejoicing before his face” (Prov 8.30). And this again shows that the Son is not foreign, but proper to the Father's essence.

Πότε οῦν ἧν ὅτε οὐκ ἔχαιρεν ὁ Πατήρ; Ει δὲ ἀεὶ ἔχαιρεν, ἀεὶ ἧν ἐν φ̓͂ͅ ἔχαιρεν Ἐν τίνι δὲ ὁ Πατὴρ χαίρει, ἡ βλέπων ἑαυτὸν ἐν τῇ ἰδίᾳ εἰκόνι, ἥτις ἐστὶν ὁ Λόγος αὐτοῦ; Εὶ δὲ καὶ ἐν οίοῖς ἀνθρώπων ηύφραίνετο τὴν οἰκουμένην σοντελέσας, ὡς ἐν αὐταῖς ταῖς Παροιμίαις γέγραπται, ἀλλὸ καὶ τοῦτο τὴν αὐτὴν ἔχει διάνοιαν. Ηὐφραίνετο γὰρ καὶ οὕτως, οὐκ ἐπιγενομένης αὐτῷ χαρᾶς, ἀλλὰ πάλιν βλέπων κατὰ τὴν ἕαυτοῦ εἰκόνα
2. For behold, it was not because of us that he came to be, as impious men say, nor is he at all from nothing — for God did not obtain for himself a cause of delighting from without —, rather what is said (provides) knowledge of that which is his own and alike. When was it then that the Father was delighting? But if he was always delighting, there always was also the one in whom he was delighting. And in whom does the Father delight, except as seeing himself in his own image, which is his Word? 3. And though “in sons of men he also rejoiced, when he finished the universe” (Prov 8.31), as it is written in the Proverbs, yet this too has the same meaning. For even in this way he rejoiced not because delight was added to him, but again on seeing the works that came to be after his image. Therefore, even in this way,


[p. 738] {#po.56.p738}
бываѭжшта дѣла. тѣмже єже и сице радовати҄҇ боу. Обраꙁа єго вина єсть. какожє и сынъ веісєлитьсѧ, нє видѧ ли сєбє въ отцы. равно бо єⷭ҇ и сє ꙗкожє и єжє глаголєть. видѣвъи мєнє, виідѣлъ єсть отьца. и аꙁъ въ отци, и отьць вꙑ мнѣ · γενόμενα τὰ ἔργα· ὥστε καὶ τὸ οὕτω χαίρειν τὸν Θεὸν τῆς εἰκόνος αὐτοῦ τὴν πρόφασιν εἶναι. Πῶς δὲ καὶ ὁ Υἱὸς εὐφραίνεται, ἣ βλέπων έαυτὸν ἐν τῷ Πατρί; Ἰσον γάρ ἐστι καὶ τοῦτο τῷ λέγειν· Ὁ ἐμε ἕωρακὼς, ἐώρακε τὸν Πατέρα, καὶ, Ἐγὼ ἐν τῷ Πατρὶ, καὶ ὁ Πατὴρ ἐν
when God was delighting, his image was the cause (of this). And how too does the Son rejoice, if not when seeing himself in the Father? For this is the same as to say, “Anyone who saw me, has seen the Father” (Jn 14.9) and “I am in the Father and the Father in me” (Jn 14.10).


[p. 739] {#po.56.p739}
Нова оубѡ вашѧ похвала. вьсѫдоуі ꙗвисѧ ѡо хрїсторатьници. и беꙁ оума оурѫігастєсѧ и ꙁвѧгостє вьсьдє. єжє г҃ь съꙁъда мѧ. начатъкъ пѫтии своихъ. протлъкѫѭште раꙁоумъ єго. и пачє своє оумышлєιниє, нєжє оумъ саломѡнъ исповѣдаѭште.| Κενὸν ὑμῶν ἄρα τὸ καύχημα πανταχόθεν δέδεικται, ὦ χριστομάχοι. καὶ μάτην ἐνεπομπεύσατε καὶ τεθρυλήκατε πανταχοῦ τὸ, Κύριος ἕκτισε με ἀρχὴν ὅδῶν αὐτοῦ, παρεξηγούμενοι τὴν διάνοιαν αὐτοῦ καὶ μᾶλλον τὴν ἑαυτῶν ἐπίνοιαν, ἣ τὸν νοῦν τοῦ Σολομῶνος ἀπαγγέλλοντες
4. Novel [Vain gr.] then is your praise as it appears in all respects, O Christ-fighters, and you mindlessly scandalize and herald everywhere the statement, “The Lord created me a beginning of his ways” (Prov 8.22), misinterpreting its meaning and professing your own thinking rather than the mind of Solomon.


[p. 740] {#po.56.p740}
бо съмышлєниє вашє ꙗвисѧ привидѣниєт тъчиѭ. рєчєноє жє въ притчахъ. и вса прѣдърєчєнаѧ, оукаꙁаѭть нє сѫштѫ ни сѫтьствоімъ ни єстьствомъ ꙁъданиоу сыноу. нъ свои порѡдъ отьцоу. прѣмѫдрость и слово истово.| имьже вьсє бысть. и бєꙁ неlго не бысть ничєсожє. Ἰδοὺ γὰρ τὸ μὲν ὑμῶν φρόνημα δέδεικται μόνον φαντασία· τὸ δὲ ἐν ταῖς Παροιμίαις ῥητὸν, καὶ πάντα τὰ προειρημένα δείκνυσι μὴ εῖναι τηρ͂ φύσει καὶ τῇ οὐσίᾳ κτίσμα τὸν Υἱὸν, ἀλλ᾽ ἵδιον γέννημα τοῦ Πατρὸς σοφίαν καὶ Λόγον ἀληθινὸν, διʼοῦ τὰ πάντα ἐγένετο, καὶ χωρὶς αὐτοὺ ἐγένετο οὐδὲ ἐν.
For behold, your thinking appears to be only a fantasy, but what is said in the Proverbs, and everything said before this, shows that the Son is not a creature in nature and essence, but is proper offspring of the Father, true Wisdom and Word, “by whom everything came to be, and without him nothing came to be” (Jn 1.3).


[p. 741] {#po.56.p741}
